in writing. the european council has adopted a resolution on the situation in the middle east. we voted in favour because the resolution clearly states that the agreement now being negotiated between the member states must be subject to the applicable eu law. it also states that all member states, whether they have adopted the euro or not, will have the right to participate in the meetings of the heads of state or government of the contracting parties, which is an important issue for us. however, we are opposed to the demands for a financial transaction tax and the introduction of eurobonds.
in writing. − the agreement on a fiscal union creates parallel structures, which worries us, among other things, because of the problems this may create, such as shortcomings in democratic control, legal uncertainty and a multiplication of supranational structures. quick decisions over the heads of the people, without anchoring and discussion in parliament or with the people, do not solve the crisis but increase the distance between the people and their politicians. but while we agree with some of the criticisms of the international agreement that the resolution raises, we cannot support the resolution as a whole because we cannot support the solutions proposed, such as deeper economic convergence, stability bonds and a fiscal union. the crisis is bigger than the euro, it is a crisis of the whole economic system on which the eu is based, and the proposals do not solve the basic problems of this system but can at best only postpone the problems for the future. growth-enhancing measures based on borrowed money lead to a constant spiral of increased lending, increased interest payments and thus increasing demands on growth to pay interest and create jobs. this inexorably leads to repeated crises and collapses, with severe social consequences in the event of collapses and increased resource consumption as the economy grows. we therefore abstain.

the rapporteur. - mr president! - what is it? commissioner, what is it? it was with great humility that i took up the task of drawing up this report, the first annual tax report. i would like to thank the shadow rapporteurs from all the political groups for a constructive discussion of this difficult but important subject. i have tried to incorporate comments from all the political groups and i think we have been able to reach a great deal of agreement. today i hope that this report will receive broad support in this house. taxation and tax policy are subjects that affect people. tax policy creates emotions both positive and negative. there are conflicts of objectives in tax policy, and a balance must always be found between them. we know it's not the easiest thing. for decades, tax policy has been regarded as an exclusively national matter, in which the eu plays no role. however, that time has passed. taxation issues must also be discussed at eu level as a consequence of globalisation. the report aims to examine areas where further tax coordination in some areas perhaps even tax harmonisation may be necessary, and where the eu can play an important role. a comprehensive and sustainable fiscal consolidation is necessary, as the ongoing debt crisis is also a fiscal crisis. the lack of coordination of tax policies is contributing to the deepening of the crisis. the necessary but painful debt reduction requires spending cuts, increased public savings and also tax increases. this report focuses on areas where the eu can do more to improve the functioning of the internal market and to create a more competitive europe. unfortunately, businesses and citizens in the eu still face tax obstacles. we must remove these obstacles if we are to create economic growth and more jobs. to do that, we need, among other things, to reduce the tax on labour and raise it on consumption. i also hope that the report will put pressure on the member states to adopt the tax proposals put forward by the commission, including the introduction of a common corporate tax base, the free ccctb. we need to solve the tax problems that people face in their everyday lives, for example if they live in one country and work in another and suffer from double taxation. tax treaties between member states are not sufficient in themselves to eliminate double taxation. this is due to the lack of consistent interpretation of the agreements between the countries. several cases of double taxation are now before the committee on petitions. an example is an italian truck driver working in germany who was taxed in both italy and germany. he was required to pay a total of 60% of his salary in tax, even though he would have been taxed only in germany and would have paid 25% in tax. this is despite the fact that there is a tax treaty between germany and italy and despite the fact that the tax authorities have established that this is wrong. as far as i know, the case is still unsolved. in this area, a common eu policy and common legislation are needed to make a difference. i therefore welcome the commission's recent adoption of a communication on double taxation, which also presents concrete measures. tax evasion, fraud and lack of transparency cost the eu and its member states many billions of euros in lost tax revenue every year. i am particularly concerned about the use of loopholes in the european regulatory framework, whereby international companies operating in the eu try to avoid paying taxes within the union. the ambition must be that every tax evasion which is deliberately withheld from the state in the future through clever tax structures must be paid back to the tax authorities. i would also like to say in conclusion that paragraph 11, on fiscal federalism, has revealed a certain amount of confusion. let me read the original proposal in english, which was submitted by my colleagues from the basque country and catalonia, among others: believes that fiscal federalism is the best tool in order to achieve self-responsibility in tax management at regional level and so entails higher economic efficiency. that refers to paragraph 11.
in writing. - at the european parliament's session in brussels on 2 february, we moderates and christian democrats voted in favour of the resolution on the statements made by the european council on 30 january. we support the resolution, with the exception of the proposals on the joint issuance of debt and the introduction of a financial transaction tax. what we need now is budgetary discipline and structural reforms to boost growth, both in the member states and in the eu as a whole.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' in the definition of the term 'competent authority'. first, the wording of that paragraph gives an incorrect picture of the commitments actually entered into under the covenant. the pact (article 16) does not say that it is to be incorporated into the eu treaties, but rather that its substance is to be incorporated into the eu legal framework. secondly, we cannot support the general call in paragraph 9 for a revision of the lisbon treaty to address weaknesses. such a review is neither desirable nor necessary. we also question the realism of the rapid process for the debt relief fund advocated in paragraph 5.
in writing. − at the brussels european parliament session on 2 february, we moderates and christian democrats voted in favour of the resolution on the conclusions of the european council of 30 january 2012. we support the resolution, with the exception of the proposals on the joint issuance of debt and the introduction of a financial transaction tax. what we need now is budgetary discipline and structural reforms to boost growth, both in the member states and in the eu as a whole.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' in the definition of a public authority. we do not think it is a good idea for europe to introduce this tax itself without the united states and other financial centres doing the same. we believe that there is a great risk that the stabilizing effects of the financial market, which are hoped to be brought about by a financial transaction tax, will not be achieved if the eu independently imposes such a tax. the risk that stock, bond and option trading will instead shift to less transparent and less open markets outside europe is too great. the proposal to introduce a european financial transaction tax is too risky.
in writing. − sport and sports are important elements in the lives of many people. we wholeheartedly support the initiatives set out in this report, such as the focus on fair competition rules and the fight against doping and betting fraud. however, not all worthy initiatives can be financially supported by the eu budget, and in times of continued economic difficulties, we believe that the demand for an ambitious budget for the sports sector in the next long-term eu budget sends the wrong signal. a responsible parliament must carefully weigh such a priority against other, equally important and laudable, priorities in order to determine how best to use taxpayers' money. we therefore vote against paragraph 1 of the report.
in writing. - the report by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on culture, youth, education and the media, on the european dimension of sport; my view is that sport is a national concern and in sweden the swedish national sport federation has a great deal of self-determination and is independent. with eu programmes and the introduction of a 'special and ambitious budget for sport policy', sport is moving towards becoming a more supranational activity rather than an independent and important actor in civil society. proposals for european coach training in the framework of lifelong learning programmes, measures to finance sport through lotteries, the partial payment of agent fees and eu flags on sportswear are some examples of measures which i consider to be completely outside the 'political boundaries'.
in writing. the report also calls for the eu to take action to combat violence against women and children, and for the eu to support the efforts of the member states to combat violence against women and children. the various forms of violence against children and their mothers are a long-term blow to the basic functions of society and are a major reason for the systematic violence against women. the report proposes a future possibility of establishing an eu special representative, similar to the un, to work on women and security issues.

member of the commission. mr president, my first words will be to express my very personal and warm thanks to mr antonio masip hidalgo for the remarkable work he has done on this regulation and for the role he has personally played in the agreement reached with the council a few days ago, on 19 december. i would also like to welcome the efforts of other members of the committee on legal affairs, including the non-attached rapporteurs, and the opinion rapporteurs of the committee on culture and education and the committee on the internal market and consumer protection, sabine verheyen and adam bielan. it is all these efforts, and others that i cannot name, that have made it possible to reach this balanced, positive compromise, which i want to support, mr hidalgo. i would also like to express my gratitude to the polish presidency for supporting this dossier until the approval i have just mentioned. ladies and gentlemen, as the rapporteur, mr hidalgo, has said, the protection of intellectual property is very important to me. it's an important issue for our economy. this is a major challenge we must face together for competitiveness. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. today is the time of the internet, of course, and of europe. that is why, on 24 may, i launched a comprehensive action plan to modernise the rules currently governing the protection of intellectual property in europe. the creation of this european observatory on infringements of intellectual property rights - it will therefore be called, mr hidalgo, as parliament has requested, the european observatory on infringements of intellectual property rights - i agree with this name - is a major initiative in this direction. this is the first legislative initiative of this action plan. the observatory will be one of the pillars of our common fight against these offences. the creation of this observatory represents two important developments: firstly, it will be a meeting place and a forum for exchange between technical experts from the public and private sectors; and secondly, it will provide a new capacity to obtain reliable data on the real impact of these offences on the economy and jobs in europe. it is important to understand the phenomenon so that we can react appropriately. i would like to thank parliament for supporting our proposal in the negotiations with the council, particularly when it came to ensuring that the information and data are properly provided by the states to the observatory. and thanks to this agreement, the observatory will become fully operational within the office for harmonization in the internal market as soon as possible. this will not, ladies and gentlemen, mark the end of all our efforts and cooperation. my departments will remain fully involved in all the observatory's activities, in a spirit of cooperation with the office. the commission will also be one of the main beneficiaries of the services provided by the observatory, due to the increased credibility and objectivity of the data it will collect. the regulation will also give the commission the possibility of referring the office for recommendations in the areas dealt with by the observatory. i hope, ladies and gentlemen, that parliament will remain very closely involved in the work of the observatory, as we have proposed, as you have asked and as i have reminded you, no later than friday afternoon when, mr hidalgo, i was in alicante for a whole day to greet the 1 200 people who work at the office of trademarks and designs. they are doing a remarkable, extremely modern job and it is in alicante, thanks to you and this agreement, that the work of this observatory, which we are thus creating thanks to your decision and your work, will be financially and technically supported. i just wanted to say thank you so much. it is very important, ladies and gentlemen, that we have a good knowledge of these phenomena and these offences, so that we can act correctly and not in the wrong way. now this is the tool that we're going to put in place, that's starting to be put in place, and that's going to be possible because of you.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, the main problem with intellectual property policy, both at eu level and in most of the member states, is that it is almost completely based on what industry lobbyists are claiming, which is founded on very little proper research. there are very many reports on intellectual property that have been compiled by the lobbyists basically taking one big number out of thin air, multiplying it by another big number and saying: ‘wow, we lose this amount of money’ (or jobs, or whatever). one prime example of such a report is the terrorism report, which was actually mentioned in the commission’s communication on this observatory. so i am very concerned about this observatory, but many people – including people from the ohim – have said ‘no, the observatory will start collecting proper academic research’. if that actually is the case, i think it would be extremely valuable, because there is a lot of very good academic research showing that the issue of intellectual property and intellectual property infringements is not necessarily as simple as the lobbyists are saying, far from it. to give the proposal the benefit of the doubt – despite my misgivings about it – hopefully the observatory can be something good if it really focuses on proper academic research. for that reason, i will be abstaining rather than voting against this proposal.
for the alde group. president. - the debate is closed. the world today is in the midst of a technological revolution which has been substantially beneficial for the citizens of europe. the internet provides services like unlimited access to music, movies and books with just a few keystrokes, and someone living in the countryside where i grew up, in kalix in northern sweden, has the same access to cultural expressions as someone living in cultural megacities like paris or london. the basic principles underlying our copyright legislation must remain valid in the digital age, but we must also dare to see that the new age means that some old systems must be modernised and simplified. collective rights management is one such system, which needs to be adapted to the 21st century. the digital cultural service providers of tomorrow must have a simpler life than today. it is not reasonable for a small company to have to hire legions of lawyers and negotiate rights in 27 countries for a long time in order to offer access to a wide repertoire of european works. we must recognise that european solutions are needed to break down barriers to trade and open up opportunities for entrepreneurs. big changes like the ones we've just been through create nervousness and, i suppose, a reflex to hold on to what you have instead of creating and reaching for something better. that is my interpretation of the commission's tardiness in this matter. the past process has been too long and europe must now have political leadership that shows that we can do more and we can do better. i therefore expect more from you, commissioner barnier, and assure you that i and the alde group in all its essentials will contribute in a constructive and pragmatic way to the development of appropriate proposals for a functioning digital single market for the 21st century.

on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, everyone agrees, it seems, that in the future common fisheries policy long-term management plans should be the basis of management. this failure to come up with a solution for anchovy and for horse mackerel – for two years now the institutions have not really come up with a solution – could be interpreted by citizens as proof that the eu institutions are not mature enough or efficient enough to deal with even such relatively simple questions. these issues need to be resolved for the eu to be credible in its reform of fisheries policies. we need to put this dispute on competences into perspective in relation to the real issue here, which is that we have the responsibility to defend and optimise what is really a public good – fish and the marine environment. this leads me to the second topic of this debate, the contribution the cfp makes to the common good. let us be honest here. technology advances all the time and fishermen become more and more efficient at fishing, which means we need fewer fishermen today to catch the same amount of fish as were caught yesterday. employment in the catching sector in europe today accounts for less than 0.1% of the total eu labour force and half of these licensed fishermen are only fishing part-time. any increased employment in the sector must involve things other than being more efficient at fishing. this could be ‘pesca-tourism’, it could mean adding value to catches in different ways, but one thing is for sure, if increased employment is what we want then we cannot promote more efficient fishing methods, including giving funds to fuel-efficient engines. i would also like to point out that it is a proven fact that over-fishing promotes algal blooms in europe, causing lots of problems for different sectors. fish in aquaculture pens die because of it, fishing gear gets clogged by it, and tourists seek other destinations when they cannot swim because of the green pea soup. a low estimate puts costs in europe caused by algal bloom at eur 177 million per year. when we speak about fish as a common good it is very important to keep in mind the different functions of wild fish in our oceans. one is to provide food, but another much more important one is that fish are crucial agents in the marine ecosystems. when we deplete stocks, water quality suffers and thereby many other economic sectors in europe, such as tourism, do so too.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the trade agreement with morocco on fruit and vegetables is much more important than it sounds. few things have hurt the economies of north africa as much as the eu's tariffs on fruit and vegetables, with unemployment and poverty as their consequences. we are their biggest market and we have high tariffs on some of their most important exports. the minimum prices we are applying here are the most cynical of all, because the more productive they are in their production, the higher the duties they face. it's an effective way to keep them on the mat, to keep them in poverty. this agreement is therefore an important step away from an old policy. let's not forget that the arab spring was just started by a fruit and vegetable trader who killed himself in protest. i therefore hope that this agreement is only the first step, that the commission will move forward and that we will soon have agreements with tunisia, libya, egypt and perhaps one day also syria. it is not enough to talk loudly about helping, we need a helping hand, and that helping hand is probably best served in this case by lowering the tariffs. that's how we helped the balkans after the war: with lower tariffs to trade for prosperity. we will also help the countries of north africa, trade for prosperity.
rapporteur. − madam president, through the decision on european radio spectrum policy we are taking necessary and crucial steps to regain european global leadership regarding mobile telephones, with all the opportunities that provides for the european telecoms industry as well as for new services and new jobs and growth. it is important to underline that this is what this decision is about: not only telecoms but also giving new dynamism to the european economy. i would like to thank the commissioner, the hungarian and polish presidencies and the shadow rapporteurs for the results. for quite a long time, europe had the leadership in mobile telecoms. it was a common european gsm standard together with european deregulation that paved the way for a dynamic european telecoms industry with new innovations, where new technolgies created growth and prosperity. we have lost that momentum and we can today see a new global economy with new actors – china, india – competing together with us and the us. we need to make the necessary efforts to lead new development not only because of the telecoms industry as such, but also because of the need to recover competitiveness. at the same time, we have seen the demand for mobile data traffic really explode, and it will continue to increase extremely rapidly, with all the opportunities for new services. my ambition in the report on this decision was that, within a multi-annual spectrum programme, we should try to take the lead regarding this second wave of internet revolution which we are facing. i want us to lead the process of change and i want us to create the best opportunities in europe regarding competitiveness and the use of telecoms opportunities. to phrase it very simply: i want europe to be number one, not number two, not number three and not number four. however, if we are to be number one, we must take the decisions which can make us number one. i am therefore very glad that we have been able to raise our ambitions in this process. thanks to this decision we will free up the 800 mhz band from january 2013, which will provide european telecoms industries and new services with the capacity which is needed. with this decision we set the target of freeing up, no later than 2015, a total of 1 200 mhz for wireless services, which will assure that we can meet the increasing demands and be in the lead for quite a long time, offering the best possible capacities for european industries, be it the service sector or the telecoms industry as such. we have also agreed that in 2015 the commission will assess the need for a further freeing-up of frequencies in order to secure that we have the best opportunities. we have decided that we shall create a european inventory and assure that we have all the necessary opportunities for wi-fi in other areas. so, all in all, madam president, what we have before us is a decision which will provide us with opportunities not only to achieve leadership in telecoms but also to increase competitiveness and opportunities for new services. i thank you all for your cooperation and look forward to the debate here tonight and the decision tomorrow.
vice-president of the commission. − madam president, there are a couple of intriguing questions. one specific point was made by marietje schaake and by ms băsescu, talking about an unlicensed spectrum for wi-fi. this is very important, there is no doubt about that, and we will examine that. that is also linked to the point that was touched upon by ms zuber: indeed, public access will be part of the inventory, as it were. it is not all about licensed and commercial users. my final conclusion so far is that i am pleased to note – i hope i may be so bold as to say this – that all the members agree on the absolute importance of increased access to spectrum as a means to provide growth and give a push to jobs. it is very important that we all agree on that issue: that the role of radio spectrum boosts growth in europe and provides real and tangible benefits for european consumers; and that the radio spectrum programme to be adopted by parliament tomorrow will not only give a good feeling after all the hard work by parliament, council and the commission, but is indeed one of those tokens whereby we can prove to the outside world that there are positive messages too. i want to thank all of you for your constructive collaboration and work during the whole procedure. time flies, a lot of blood, sweat and tears have been shed, but in the end only the result counts. i think that a wonderful job has been done by all of you.
rapporteur. − madam president, spectrum is limited but the opportunities are unlimited, as we have seen with developments in the past and as we will see with the development that we are now facing. i think it is fair to say that media and culture will have more opportunities than ever to reach the audience, as we see is happening just now. things are changing, and i think the most important lesson to learn from this debate is that the enormous, rapid development we have seen in the past decade will be just as rapid and surprising in the coming decade. it is important for us to secure the full capacity to take advantage of all this, for media and culture as well as for new services. that is why i think it is also important, regarding the next steps, to secure the rapid implementation of this legislation, but at the same time to prepare for the next steps, because we have only seen the beginning of a new development, of a second wave of internet revolution. this will have its consequences and opportunities, as we have seen in the demonstrations in northern africa, and as we have seen in the revolution in different parts of our economies. the faster we are, the more use we can make of the next steps. we have decided to set the target of 1200 mhz; that is for 2015. after that we should assess the needs and proceed rapidly in the next few years, because the future is coming closer and closer and we need to be in the lead. in order to be in the lead, we need to take the lead, and proceed by being the first to open up new opportunities all the time, because they are unlimited while the resource, the spectrum, is limited.
mr president, i would like to thank you. we must do something about the lack of matching in the european labour market. since 2010, we have not only skyrocketing unemployment, but also a sharply increasing labor shortage. employers are finding it increasingly difficult to find the skills they are looking for. this is ominous. unless strong action is taken, high unemployment will become permanent and competitiveness will be eroded. most governments are too passive in improving matching. firstly, a more active labour market policy is needed, whereby all those who become unemployed are given the opportunity to directly improve their skills to match available jobs, and secondly, lifelong learning must be put into practice. everyone must be given the opportunity to learn at all stages of life. broad education initiatives covering school, higher education, vocational training and further training are needed to ensure that the workforce continuously matches the increasingly knowledge-intensive jobs that need to be created. there must be a new way of thinking in this area.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the existing aid scheme to cover the costs of the new scheme, which will be implemented in the next five years. the programme was introduced at a time when there were large stocks of agricultural products and it was considered a just solution to distribute these foodstuffs to those in need. as the eu's agricultural policy has been adapted to the market, these stocks have been reduced and the possibility of stocking raw materials with eu funds has been abolished for several products. we would have liked to see other forms of support for the most vulnerable in society, as the eu and the member states must take responsibility, not only for the economic institutions, but also for their people in times of economic crisis.
in writing. the commission is also proposing that the european parliament should be consulted on the draft budget for the financial year 2000 and on the draft budget for the financial year 2000 and on the draft budget for the financial year 2000 and on the draft budget for the financial year 2000. these are plans which are at this stage only at the conceptual stage and which need to be worked through very carefully before they can possibly be put into practice. at the same time, as representatives of the electorate of a non-euro country, we have no interest in preventing the euro countries from moving quickly in these areas if they consider it necessary. as long as the single market and wider eu cooperation are not adversely affected, it must be up to the euro countries themselves to choose the solutions they consider appropriate to manage sovereign debt and save the single currency.
in writing. − it is our conviction that a system of permanent joint liability for sovereign debt is no short-term solution for the euro area. there are a number of issues concerning moral hazard, incentives, impacts on interest rates and legal constraints that, to date, have not been investigated properly by the commission. when the debt crisis has been resolved in the euro area and the member states have reduced their budget deficits, such a system could be evaluated. there are, however, necessary strict preconditions that have to be met for a common issuance of bonds, for example a culture of stability must have been credibly established in the euro area and a sustainable fiscal framework needs to be in place, aimed at ensuring both enhanced economic governance and economic growth in the euro area.
in writing. − today we voted in favour of the resolution on employment and social aspects of the 2012 annual growth survey. we would point out, however, that higher tax outcomes are good and that actively fighting discrimination is good, but that these are subsidiarity issues. we are also opposed to the demand for a financial transaction tax.

mr president, i would like to thank you. i would also like to thank the rapporteur, mr derek vaughan, who i think is doing an extremely serious and creative job with parliament's own budget and the administrative budgets of the other institutions. i also note that he has been praised by virtually all the other political groups, which shows that he has succeeded in creating a broad consensus on where we stand. it is indeed worth noting that there is a new attitude. so far parliament's budget has increased every year, but now there is a broad consensus that we should freeze the budget at the global level and that we should make big cuts in certain individual areas. there is also a great deal of agreement that we should start being restrictive with regard to members' own costs. i think that's very important. the report has had a real impact, as we can see from the fact that only seven amendments have been tabled, which i believe is a record. if we look at the figures, we can see that whereas traditionally we have always been at 20% of the long-term budget level for administrative expenditure, we are now down to about 19%, and that is actually a saving of about 70 million euros. this is, of course, due to pressure from the outside world, but the point is that we are not just cutting back. what we need to do above all is to find a way forward, which is about efficiency, modernisation, economy - and i think we are only at the beginning. i believe this can be done without compromising the quality of the core business. we must, of course, defend the legislative and budgetary work in particular, but this requires a lot of systematic work. i am therefore very pleased that we have been able to reach agreement on the creation of a working group in parliament with the possibility of involving external expertise in areas where we can learn a lot: buildings, maintenance, energy, the environment, procurement, travel and so on. these are areas where we can learn from both business and public administration. in areas such as the organisation of legislative work, interpretation, translation and so on, i am convinced that we can learn from other parliaments and from other international organisations. then, of course, this parliament has costs that are not comparable - travel, interpretation, etc. - because this is the only multinational legislative parliament in the world and we must of course take that into account. finally, and i am not speaking as the coordinator of my group, but as a swedish social democrat, if we are to be respected for the way we manage the trust of citizens and taxpayers' money, we cannot continue to move parliament between brussels and strasbourg in the long term. so i appeal to the governments now pushing to cut the eu budget: please put this question on the agenda seriously.
for the alde group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the family is the one that can give a person security, belonging and security in life. if for any reason you have to leave both your country and your family, you always carry within you the dream of being part of the family again, of being reunited, of being together. that hope never dies. the commission has now reviewed the implementation of the 2003 directive on the right to family reunification and found that many member states are having problems making it work in practice. i therefore welcome new, clearer guidelines from the commission to the member states. the provisions are in many respects vague and new guidelines can help to safeguard the existing provisions and be a good tool for the member states, but i am against the introduction of new provisions and i strongly question the timing of the commission's choice to release this green paper on the future of family reunification. if we open up to the adoption of new regulations, i think there is a risk that the outcome will be worse than it is today and that fewer people will be reunited with their families. i would like to hear how the commission intends to deal with the implementation of this directive, which several member states are doing so badly. have any infringement procedures been initiated? if infringement proceedings have not been initiated, why not? what's the excuse? let us also remember that in fact only one member state, namely the netherlands, has asked for an amendment to the directive, and more than that should be required to open up new provisions. we all have the right to family life through international human rights instruments, and this right to live together, to love and to belong together should not be jeopardized by any of us. the directive must provide guarantees to protect the right to belong and to be a human being together with other human beings in a family.

in writing. - we swedish social democrats cannot support a trade agreement which is contrary to international law. that is why we voted against this agreement between the eu and morocco. the eu can only conclude agreements with the occupying power of morocco that include western sahara territory if certain strict conditions are met. these conditions that the agreement should be in accordance with the will and interest of the occupied people are not fulfilled. the legal service of the european parliament therefore considers that the compatibility of this fta with the international agreements binding on the eu and its member states cannot be guaranteed. when the european parliament asked the european commission whether products from western sahara should be covered by the agreement, the commission replied [i] the extent to which exports of products from western sahara actually benefit from the trade preferences. both efta and the united states have trade agreements with morocco which do not affect western sahara. the eu should of course respect international law in the same way.
in writing. the european parliament has also called for the establishment of a european union-wide system of origin and origin declarations. we support the eu's commitment to improving trade relations with the mediterranean countries and to creating effective and fair trade rules. but we cannot ignore the fact that the eu has encountered a number of problems in applying the rules of origin to products originating in the israeli settlements in the occupied palestinian territories. the problem of determining the true origin of products exported from israel must be solved in a better way. the risk is that, if no effective solution is found, this could become a tool for the expansion of this abuse. goods cumulated by third countries with israel must respect the same territorial principles as the eu. this is also in line with the judgment of the court of justice of the european union in the so-called uk case. in addition, it is unclear how the agreement deals with goods from western sahara.

the s&d group is in favour of the proposal. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. in the light of all that happens in a year, even major disasters such as the one in fukushima may be forgotten. focus is shifting. we, the decision-makers, are concentrating on other crises and problems, while the media's eyes are on something else. it is therefore very good and important that we in the european parliament, among others, should now pay attention to the anniversary of the fukushima disaster. what happened on march 11, 2011, was a disaster that few could have foreseen. it was an earthquake, a tsunami, and nuclear meltdowns that would lead to the almost immediate death of more than 19,000 people and the displacement of 325,000. the small town of ukedo, just north of fukushima, was completely destroyed. many of the residents there also died. this city is no longer habitable. its population has been dispersed and now lives in surrounding towns. but there is still a city. it remains in survivors and in relationships between people from workplaces, from schools, from neighborhoods, but as time goes on, these bonds and these networks also fade. nothing will be left. fukushima was not only a human tragedy, but this disaster of course revived the debate on nuclear energy. the main lesson that i think we can learn from fukushima is this simple, but sometimes difficult, lesson: the future is not in nuclear power. nuclear technology will certainly remain in various forms for a considerable time, but unless states actively choose to build nuclear power through political choices, it is simply not viable. the market is not investing in nuclear power. nuclear power is entirely dependent on subsidies. nuclear power is thus becoming more expensive, while renewable energy is becoming cheaper. it's as simple as that. each country has the right to decide on its own energy sources. however, the political responsibility now lies with us in the eu, with us in the member states, to show that an energy future can be made possible by renewable energy. in this area we have a great challenge. alternative and renewable energy sources are not seen today as sufficiently developed, as efficient, as broad enough, as something for ordinary industry, for ordinary societies, for ordinary citizens. if we are to do that, it will require a huge investment in research, in development, in infrastructure, in actually developing this energy source in cooperation between the market and society. we are holding this debate to remember and honor the victims of fukushima. we also have it as a springboard for the future. what we need to show is that there are lessons to be learned from history.

we cannot just keep talking and talking about change, but now we need action. in many cases, legislative action will be needed to address this. one way is to introduce different forms of quotas, if necessary. but as sophia in 't veld said, it may not be what you start with, but at some point you have to address this issue. some people find quotas outrageous. yes, i agree. i'm upset about today's male quota. it is silent and informal, but nevertheless men are quoted at the expense of women. some even say that it is bad for women to be elected because of their gender, but it is rather that today's male quota is harmful to men, because people assume that many men are elected just because they are men. in the name of democracy, we must therefore have a system where the experiences of women and men can be exploited much, much more clearly and reflected in the parliamentary assemblies. i would therefore like to vote in favour of sirpa pietikäinen's report.

for the alde group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. within the european institutions, we have gradually built up an understanding of each other's differences. this is most obvious in our choice of language. united in diversity. it has been absolutely essential for european integration that we have discussed and thought together about how to tackle different ways of solving problems and understanding each other's differences. only through the exchange of experience can we learn to look beyond differences and instead try to find what increases respect for each other's differences. i am convinced that this also applies to the legal professions - judges, prosecutors and lawyers. it is easier to build trust between the judicial systems of the member states if there are face-to-face meetings, meetings where people can talk, have a dialogue and also educate each other in face-to-face meetings. only in this way can respect and knowledge be built and differences be bridged. an important part of ensuring that the rules we decide here have the intended effect throughout our member states is that we provide opportunities for increased exchange and discussion among those who are called upon to interpret our laws and regulations. i therefore believe that this debate at this late hour is a proposal which will take us further and closer to a common understanding of what european legal culture is and can be in the future.
mr president, i would like to thank you. keeping the eu together despite the tensions between regions and countries is now the most important task for the union's leaders. president sarkozy should reconsider and not challenge nationalist sentiments. history should have taught him a lesson. the european parliament's annual tax report, for which i was responsible, states that broad and sustainable fiscal consolidation is necessary, but we must also focus on growth. it is about how we can create more growth in europe and thus more employment at national and eu level. it is particularly important that we look at how we can improve the internal market and create a more competitive europe. combating youth unemployment, promoting growth and the conditions for small business and breaking down tax barriers are key. we risk leaving a whole generation in europe without hope for a better future. let me end by saying that when listening to mr farage, mr eppink, mr gollnisch and the others, i think that sometimes they ought to remember what european history is all about. i think that we in this chamber ought to remember this. previously in history, mr. gollnisch, we solved the problem on the battlefield. we don't do that now. i listen to you, you listen to me, and i think that is the way we should all prefer the history of europe. mr gollnisch, i think you are damaging the european idea of working together, and it is not our fault; i think you should also consider your own responsibility.
mr president, if the problem of the european economy were that our public spending is too low and our deficits too small, we would have no problems at all. i want to say this in response to the debate here, as it sounds as if we could solve our problems by increasing public spending and increasing the deficits, thereby achieving social cohesion and economic growth. we have seen the contrary: that uncontrolled public spending and big deficits are undermining social security and hindering growth, and creating the austerity of increasing interest rates. that is the reality, and those who believe that increased public spending is the solution to our problems should just look around and take a look at our problems. on the other hand what we need to do is to create a new way of stimulating the economy. i think it is important for the commission to understand that the way to social cohesion and social security does not run parallel to the policy for economic growth. economic growth and new jobs are the key issue in order to achieve social development in europe that we can all be satisfied with. that is why i would like to urge the commission to proceed with the reforms for the internal market, for competitiveness in the member states and for making the european union the biggest market in the world.

on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, there is really not much argument on the topic of this discussion tonight in plenary; those countries that are eligible to increase cofinancing of the eff have indeed severe budget constraints today, and we support this step to make it easier for them to implement their fisheries programmes and invest in the future. i think that in this context, it is important – especially for those countries that have declining areas which are very dependent on fisheries – to take the opportunity offered by this amended regulation also to review and update operational programmes and adapt in order to become competitive in the future. in our opinion, the proposal for a new european maritime and fisheries fund that will replace the current european fisheries’ fund shows the way. there is only one way for member states aiming to mitigate and reduce the risk of future crises in the fisheries sector, and that is to rebuild stocks. no young person in the european union today wants to invest their future in a business that is not sustainable but is dependent on subsidies; if there were, consumers would not accept it. fisheries have to be ecologically sustainable in order to be economically and socially sustainable in the long term. with no fish there can be no fishermen. the future emff must be designed and implemented in order to support the objectives of the cfp, especially the sustainable exploitation of marine resources. in these times of budget restraints it is even more important that the entire european maritime and fisheries fund be geared for supporting sustainability. public aid should be restricted to investments that are in the public interest. considerable amounts of money from the structural funds used to be awarded to building new vessels and modernising existing ones. in the new emff, the commission is proposing some significant changes, including the suggestion to terminate public aid for scrapping, which is still available under the current programme. the european court of auditors has criticised the scrapping aid for not having contributed to the reduction of fleet capacity. indeed, if a ship owner knows that a handsome subsidy for leaving the fleet in the future will always be available, there is good reason to stay on and hope that the situation improves. such aid is not consistent with the promotion of sustainable fishing. aid for the modernisation of fishing vessels is rarely in the public interest either, and while the idea of paying a ship owner to install a more energy-efficient engine which emits less co2 may be seductive, it is in reality impossible to ensure that the fishing capacity and effort are not increased when an engine becomes more energy efficient. significant changes to the eu fishing fleets will be needed. if they are to be achieved, public aid could help in different ways. it must, however, be limited to measures which reduce the environmental impact of the fishing operations by such means as the conversion of vessels and gear to methods which are more selective or inflict less damage to the sea floor. social support measures during the restructuring of the eu fleet will also be critical. so far, such social support has been directed too much towards ship owners and too little towards the crew, and this imbalance must be redressed through measures such as retraining. only in this way will the fishing sector be sustainable in the future.
the s&d group is in favour of the proposal. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr christian dan preda, for his excellent cooperation. it feels good to be able to send a clear signal to iceland that we look forward to bringing iceland into the eu family. it would be a good thing for a new member state to have such a strong democratic tradition and a well-functioning market economy. membership of the eea and schengen already means that iceland has close cooperation with the eu and has made good progress in preparing for accession. it is therefore gratifying that the people and parliament of iceland want to complete the accession negotiations. i hope that we can now also have a good and serious debate in iceland about what eu membership means. as a member of the committee on budgets and having led a committee delegation to iceland last year, i should also note that iceland's accession will not impose any burden on the eu budget. in many areas, i even think that the eu could learn some important lessons from iceland. i'm thinking about iceland's way out of the economic crisis. it has successfully implemented the international monetary fund's recovery programme without the unilateral austerity that has unfortunately been imposed on greece, and has successfully introduced strict controls on the financial sector. i am thinking that iceland's membership could give the eu a more active role in the arctic and thus contribute to sustainable solutions in this sensitive region. i am thinking of the eu's fisheries policy, where we could learn a lot from iceland's more sustainable fishing model in the run-up to the eu's forthcoming reform. i am thinking of energy policy, where iceland, which gets almost all its energy from renewable sources, could make a valuable contribution through its world-leading knowledge of geothermal energy in particular. however, there remains a sensitive discussion with iceland on the abolition of whaling and the sale of whale products, as well as some unresolved fisheries and agricultural issues. i look forward to welcoming iceland as a member of the eu, but i am of course well aware that the outcome of a forthcoming referendum on eu membership is far from certain. for a yes to be a yes, there must be a sensible agreement on fisheries policy.

in writing. - i have abstained from voting on this report. i think the eu made the right decision in 1988 when it banned the import of beef treated with growth promoting hormones. the wto dispute settlement body ruled in 1996 that the us and canada would be entitled to impose trade sanctions on the eu on a whole range of products, as a legitimate response to the eu decision. and so it did, leading to a dispute resolution process and a memorandum of understanding. i believe that the original wto decision against the eu is based on a free trade principle which does not care about either environmental or social consequences. that is why this whole so-called settlement of the dispute is absurd, and i choose to abstain.
in writing. - we voted in favour of the report on the broad guidelines for the 2013 eu budget because we can support the overall priorities and objectives focusing on having a budget for economic recovery and growth. however, we do not accept the report's position on the size of the budget as such. contrary to what the report says, budgetary discipline and priorities are necessary. this is not just the case for the member states, but for the eu as a whole. in this context, we voted in favour of the amendments seeking clearer priorities in the budget, control of payments, evaluation of programmes, efficiency, control and a freeze of the budget, whereby instead of larger budget lines, priority is given to re-prioritization and transfers.

in writing. - when the european parliament voted on 14 march on the guidelines for the preparation of the 2012 budget for the commission, i chose to vote in favour. this is because i support the overall objectives of a budget that is restrained and focuses on sustainability and growth. however, the report contains elements which run counter to the general guidelines. moderation is to be encouraged. in cases where more money has been requested, i have voted for amendments which seek to prevent this and have voted for clear measurable targets for disbursements to specific purposes. additional resources shall not be unnecessarily added and increased costs may be resolved by budgetary re-prioritization through transfers.
in writing. - i voted in favour of the joint resolution on diabetes because it is important to draw attention to one of the most widespread chronic health problems affecting 32 million europeans today. we need to address this problem through more knowledge, education, research and prevention. health issues should be addressed primarily at national level, but also to raise awareness of the consequences of diabetes across the eu. it is therefore important to improve cooperation between member states and to encourage the formulation of a diabetes strategy, in order to find more effective solutions to a disease that knows no borders. i particularly welcome the danish presidency's priority to this issue.

in writing. - i voted for the report. the report introduces certain restrictions and regulates derivatives trading. i believe that much stronger measures are needed. this speculative trade is one of the main causes of the financial crisis. the future of financial markets requires very strict regulation and taxation. but since the report is, after all, a step in the right direction, i choose to vote in favour.
in writing. the european parliament is presenting for the first time proposals which imply cuts in parliament's own, often too costly, expenditure. the proposal to have only one place for the european parliament to meet is particularly positive. but the proposal to freeze the overly generous contributions to the european parliament is also positive. if the european parliament is ever to win the confidence of european citizens, further austerity is required, among many other things, both in the general costs of the european parliament and in the benefits for elected members. that's why i chose to withdraw.
in writing. - i voted for the report. the crisis in the spanish construction sector is mainly due to the failure of the euro and has very little to do with globalisation. the design of the euro resulted in the same interest rate situation throughout the euro area. in spain, as in ireland, interest rates should have been much higher to counteract the overheating of the economies of these countries. instead, the interest rate policy set by the ecb contributed to the creation of housing bubbles in both countries. the bubbles burst when the financial crisis hit. the mass unemployment created in both countries and the construction sector is particularly hard hit. the fundamental problem is thus the functioning of the euro and of financial capitalism. but i do believe that the workers affected must be given all possible support to alleviate their plight. that is why i voted in favour of the report.
in writing. - i voted against the report. this is because i do not believe that further institutions and funds are needed to support the development of democracy. the eidhr can fulfil this task through certain rule changes and reforms that simplify the handling of cases. the individual member states can also develop their own democracy assistance. a doubling of this type of assistance by the eu is therefore not necessary.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'common carrier' in the context of the directive. it is good that the resolution stresses that eu-turkey relations are mutually beneficial and must be based on mutual commitment. we therefore call on turkey to continue the reform process to achieve concrete results, in particular on violence against women, freedom of expression and judicial guarantees in trials. i particularly welcome the paragraph which protects the rights of minorities. the new turkish constitution under preparation could be a historic opportunity to create a more democratic, open and inclusive society. at the same time, it is important that the eu moves forward with visa liberalisation for turkey. i welcome initiatives to harmonise document requirements to facilitate administrative procedures, but more needs to be done to unlock the situation and build trust. i fully support the commission's positive agenda, which is not an alternative path but an important complement and support to the accession negotiations. the best way to address the problems and reforms in turkey is to engage in a continuous and constructive dialogue for accession negotiations. the lack of clarity on the eu is creating tension and slowing down the reform process in turkey.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environmentally friendly use of energy. it also clearly states that shareholders are responsible for the appointment of directors and the manner in which this is to be done. these are important principles for us. the report also includes a formulation calling on the commission to come forward with legislative proposals on the quota of women on company boards. we continue to oppose all such demands because we believe it is wrong to reduce people to representatives of a particular group, be it gender, ethnicity, age or anything else, instead of looking at their individual characteristics and qualities.
in writing. - there is still a long way to go in europe for gender equality. it is discouraging that the gender balance in the boards of the largest european companies is still so skewed. but we believe that legislating quotas to force company owners to elect more women to top positions is the wrong way to go. we believe that there are other ways to break down attitudes and structures that hinder women's participation, such as skills development, mentoring and better support for women who want to pursue their careers. more needs to be done to ensure that more women reach top management positions and thus form part of the recruitment base for company boards. we also believe that the imbalance in the eu's own institutions should be addressed before we legislate on the private sector. that is why we voted against amendments nos 1 and 3 to the report on european corporate governance. however, the report contains many other good ideas and ambitions and we have therefore given our support to the report as a whole.

the committee on the internal market and consumer protection has tabled an opinion. - mr president! - what is it? a common consolidated corporate tax base is an important first step towards improving the competitiveness of european companies and a more efficient internal market. it creates more jobs, reduces business costs and eliminates unnecessary red tape. in the imco opinion we proposed changes to the commission proposal. several of these have been included in the compromise by marianne thyssen, which we welcome. this includes the establishment of a common dispute settlement forum, which was also mentioned by commissioner algirdas šemeta. in the committee's opinion we also stated that the introduction of a common consolidated corporate tax base should be voluntary. competitive tax systems and institutional competition are valuable. the system should not become mandatory until practical experience of its operation has been gained. i think that more companies than the so-called european companies would be willing to participate if the system were voluntary. it is therefore unfortunate that the compromise already involves the gradual introduction of a compulsory system. this will make it more difficult to get more member states to participate. better the carrot than the whip!
the s&d group is in favour of the proposal. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the energy tax directive is obsolete. it needs to be modernised and improved in order to function in an efficient and balanced way. the tax regime must be adapted to the ambitious increases in energy and environmental policy that have taken place since the adoption of the directive in 2003. the regulatory framework needs to be adapted to new conditions on the energy market. in particular, the discrimination against renewable fuels must end. these fuels must be given the same conditions as fossil fuels if development is to be sustainable. in addition, energy taxation needs to be coordinated with emissions trading. an ambitious revision of the directive is a necessary element of a credible energy and environment policy. without revisions, we will hardly be able to meet the eu 2020 target. without revision, we are not taking responsibility for future generations either. the outcome of the econ committee's deliberations is good. the econ report preserves the main lines of the commission's logical and well-developed proposal. the most controversial point, the principle of neutrality, requires member states to ensure that all fuels used for the same purpose are taxed in the same way. it is a great way to create fair and predictable conditions of competition. all fuels are given the same chance in a volatile energy market. this type of solid rules of the game is crucial to fostering technological development, growth and job creation. in the short term, the principle of neutrality could create problems for the automotive industry. the econ report has resolved this by a long phasing-in period until 2025 for this sector. if the principle would create fiscal imbalances, the commission may propose compensatory changes to other vehicle-related taxation. in my view, this is a model that is both rational and workable. tomorrow's vote is about the credibility of energy policy. we cannot build a sustainable economy without a modern system of energy taxation. i would also like to thank astrid lulling. we've had a good working relationship, even though we've had a few differences of opinion.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the climate policy objectives we have are not castles in the air. they are legally binding on the member states and they are clear. we have decided to reduce our greenhouse gas emissions by 20 percent by 2020. today's decision is about how we do that. all the research shows that a price on carbon dioxide provides cost-effective emissions reductions. the price of carbon dioxide is driving technological progress and the necessary social transformation. every member who voted for our climate objectives should therefore support the principles of the energy tax directive, and vice versa: anyone who rejects this directive also rejects the objectives and structure of climate policy. without this directive we will continue to live with rules that tax biofuels more heavily than fossil fuels, that do not provide cost-effective emission reductions and that risk double taxation due to the poor coordination of the emissions trading system. the proposal we have received from the econ committee is not as ambitious as it could be, but it retains the key elements of the commission's proposal for a new energy tax directive. there are livery belts and hammocks and airbags in this proposal for a gentle change in the energy tax structure. this is now just a consultation with parliament. the council decides by unanimity and what we say here has no formal significance. nevertheless, tomorrow's vote is crucial, because it is about the credibility of decisions we have already taken on environmental and climate policy. i think it is time for the european parliament to take up its responsibilities. tomorrow we have the chance to show that we are not afraid to vote when the content of the decisions is equal to zero!
mr president, the main problem, if you take a global perspective, is not the use of energy as such but emissions as regards their effect on the climate and the environment. we know that some energy fuels or sources produce lower emissions: renewables, bio-fuels, nuclear power, and that some emit more: gas, oil and coal. the problem is, of course, that if we tax the energy more than the emissions, we are creating a situation where we are hindering the emergence of competitive new renewable energy resources. the problem is that if we do not tax the environmental consequences, we get a situation – as has been mentioned in this debate – where diesel, for example, becomes more expensive than petrol. that is, of course, a problem for those who use diesel cars. but the main problem is that we have a system of taxation that is hindering the best energy sources while supporting the old energy sources. that is the problem with this proposal: it is not doing these things straight by supporting the emergence of new energy sources.
madam president, during this very interesting debate this morning, i have not heard, coming out of the wave of criticism, a single alternative proposal that could be pursued. international agreements are all about compromises. that is the nature of an agreement. you dance with each other. what is the alternative being offered to the citizens of europe? not to travel to the united states? that is the only alternative being offered. that means depriving them of their freedom. let us have a minimum of realism in this debate. no, it is not an ideal agreement. fine, but what else is there? the united states authorities can collect data in their country. it is their country that people are travelling to. would we prefer them to have secret bilateral agreements with member states? would we want them to take away visa-free travel and suspend it? they could do that. this agreement is a great improvement over that of 2007. it is a significant step forward in providing data protection to our citizens. my vote is for protection, safeguards and freedom and security for the citizens of europe.
the s&d group is in favour of the proposal. president. - the debate is closed. as rapporteur, i am proud of the progress made together with the council and the commission in adopting the new legislation on trafficking in human beings. the directive has a holistic approach, focusing on both prevention and prosecution of traffickers. it is now more than a year since the directive was adopted and i am pleased to hear from both the commission and the council here today about its implementation. a few weeks ago i was at a seminar with the eu's anti-trafficking coordinator. she said that the latest available statistics from the member states show that a clear majority, over 70 per cent, of trafficking victims are women who have been victims of sex slavery. although this is confirmed by a number of other studies, i feel that there is resistance to talking about this fact. instead of discussing sex slavery, which according to all available statistics accounts for the majority of the cases, there is a tendency to focus on exploitation in other sectors. this is deeply unfortunate as we will never get to human trafficking unless we have the courage to speak openly about all its causes. a very important part of the directive is precisely about working against demand and against the very profitability of trafficking in human beings. article 18 requires member states not only to act against trafficking in human beings but also to take appropriate measures to counter and reduce the demand underlying all forms of exploitation related to trafficking in human beings. this includes reviewing the liability of purchasers, regardless of the type of slavery they are paying for. it is therefore particularly interesting to see how the council and the commission view this in the light of the legislation. i look forward to the evaluation that will eventually come!
for the gue/ngl group. president. - the debate is closed. the most common form of trafficking is, of course, that of women and girls who are exploited sexually. so i'm going to focus on that. women and girls are being tricked and coaxed into a world of exploitation and cruelty. since the demand for women to be bought is greater than the number of prostitutes in europe, these women are recruited from other parts of the world to be bought and sold again and again. this is cynical, unworthy, colonial and even racist. we must have the courage to make decisions that question this. sex is not okay. as a man, i reject the right of men to buy women's bodies. as a politician, i say we must have the courage to take decisions that will truly abolish trafficking in women. of course, many important measures are needed, such as extending support to victims of trafficking in human beings, strengthening their rights, giving victims of trafficking in human beings the right to reside in the country where they are exploited. at the same time, we must always remember that without demand for women's bodies, there is no trafficking in women and girls. it means the real problem is with the sex buyers. i believe that we must go all the way in that chain in order to be able to restrict trade, that is to say, to be able to ban sex buying as well. it has proved to be an effective measure in norway, sweden and iceland. because it's not the women in prostitution who should be punished, it's the men who should be put in the spotlight. to sum up: combating trafficking in human beings in the eu is largely about combating trafficking in women and girls. to make that fight effective, it takes political courage to fight demand. such a policy would be much more effective than the current focus on biometric identification, passenger data and enhanced border controls.
madam president, i welcome commissioner malmström’s genuine commitment to fighting trafficking, along with all the measures that the commission has implemented already. now is the time for all the member states to do the same and implement fully, swiftly and effectively the directive that was adopted a year ago. but there is more to be done, because the figures are stunning and the stories are heartbreaking. the issue is the millions of women, children and men who live as slaves in our neighbourhoods. there is a need for a cultural paradigm shift, and the time of impunity has to come to an end. the culture of impunity, of abuse, cheating and oppression, has to be replaced by a culture which criminalises trafficking. all the perpetrators should hear our voice. they will not feel immune anywhere, in any corner of europe. we have to join forces on this, not only by better training police forces and taking into account children’s and women’s special needs as well as victim protection, but also by adding some prevention. we need to do more than just persecute; we must also really improve cooperation and have stronger sanctions. this may be a challenge for a criminal system, but we need to see adults and children freed and perpetrators in prison.

for the gue/ngl group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would first like to warmly thank nicole kiil-nielsen for her outstanding work and for her cooperation in the report. in fact, this report is historic in that, to my knowledge, it is the first time that parliament has addressed climate issues from a gender perspective. the report makes it quite clear that climate change is not gender neutral. for example, men contribute more than women to climate change, but it is women who are most affected. people's lifestyle patterns simply leave a larger ecological footprint. women are therefore more sustainable consumers, but also seem to be more willing than men to take action to preserve the environment. it is important that the report highlights the concept of climate justice, because today's injustices would be even greater if we do not tackle climate change. it would affect poor countries in general and women in particular. the millennium development goals are therefore under threat because of man's environmental degradation. it is also clear that the male quota that permeates all of society today is also present in climate change. i therefore support the report's proposals for targeted measures such as quotas to increase the representation of women at all levels of decision-making. the eu and its member states must lead by example and do much better in this area. women are key actors in the fight against climate change. several good examples show that training women saves lives in disaster management, increases biodiversity, prevents desertification and protects forests. i would like to say, in the spirit of the commission representative, that when women take part in climate action, their position in society is often strengthened. this in turn leads to increased democracy and the promotion of human rights.
mr president, i would like to thank you. this report shows how women and men are differently responsible for the climate change that is occurring, and that we have different access to our common resources and are affected differently by climate change. knowing this is essential if we are to change behaviors and attitudes and change the world. that is what most of us in the european parliament want, although perhaps not everyone is on the same page. these values and skills need to be shared in the eu and elsewhere in the world. we are negotiating trade and environmental agreements, and the gender aspect must be included. i would like to thank nicole kiil-nielsen for this report on climate policy. it takes a slightly different approach than the usual. this is good because most of us are aware that political solutions are more effective if we have knowledge and awareness of both class and gender perspectives. this also applies to climate policy. if our climate policy, which we decide on here by a large majority, is to become reality and not just empty words on paper, we need reports to guide us in the way this report can. thank you again for this work.
