mr president, i would like to thank you. i will vote in favour of this regulation, but i will do so with no enthusiasm at all because i see this as a missed opportunity. now, i've talked about this issue and we've all agreed and used the right words. we have said that data roaming is something that is really damaging to the internal market and damaging to consumers. we have also said that this is something we have to regulate, which is a pity if you do not like regulation. we've all been in complete agreement on this, so we've all used the right words, but when we got to the numbers, it was the wrong numbers. from 2014 the ceiling will be 20 cents per megabyte for data roaming. that may not sound like much per megabyte, but that's 200 euros per gigabyte. if we compare that with the national prices of any country, we see that the robbery of consumers continues. unfortunately, we're going to need the roaming 4 that paul rübig mentioned earlier. this is a step in the right direction, but it is too little and it is too slow.
madam president, i would like to thank you. today's vote, or today's decision, will mean that we drastically reduce the cost of mobile surfing, calling and texting. it will be felt in the wallets of consumers and travellers and it will be felt as early as july this year, when costs will fall by 35 per cent. next year, costs will fall by another number of percentage points, and in 2014, 65 percent of costs will be gone. the price of data is also falling substantially, and all this is clear evidence that policy can play a role: the market cannot decide, the market must not be able to charge excess prices. in a borderless single market, however, there is really no reason why calling between countries should be more expensive than calling within countries. we now have a guarantee that the commission will come back with a strong legislative proposal for a fully integrated telecoms market if not enough improvements have been made in 2016. so we're prepared to go further to make sure that people don't pay more than they actually need to.
madam president, i would like to thank the rapporteur for the work she has done and all her efforts. i think one of the important achievements in this report is the fact that now, from 2014, we will be able to have what is called ‘local breakout’, meaning that we have a real structural reform changing the logic of the telecom markets in regard to internet surfing and all those things that are more and more important today. for me it is still a paradox that in this most modern and dynamic market, the telecoms market, we still have 27 different national markets in the european union where, in reality, we should have just one market able to form the basis for our global leadership. we can discuss different price caps and we can discuss different ways of regulating how things are today, but the important thing is that, if we are opening the door for one european market in the european union, with this local breakout it will be possible for all of us to connect to the internet when we are abroad in the european union without, so to speak, phoning back home and paying these very expensive costs. we will be able to do this as we do with wi-fi. so when the danish presidency or other presidencies come here to brussels or strasbourg they will not have to get connected. we are the home country. they will be able to use the best possible offer in the country where we are and that will be the case not just for the presidencies, it will be the case for all citizens in europe. i think this achievement will also mean that we will be able to use all the opportunities that we are opening up today on the internet for all mobile data traffic and that this will lay the ground for a more competitive europe, but also a more connected europe.
for the alde group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i had the honour last november of leading the delegation from the libé committee to sicily and lampedusa to visit and inspect the centres receiving asylum seekers. before and after the visit we asked the italian authorities to provide us with information. that information has been extremely inadequate, if not completely omitted. information and facts on migration flows are important to understand events in 2011 and to ensure that the mistakes that have occurred are not repeated in the future. ngos have told us that the italian authorities under the previous government did not deal with the situation on lampedusa in a particularly humane way. the reception units for migrants on lampedusa were for a long time extremely overcrowded. people slept in the open throughout the island while transports from lampedusa to sicily were delayed and delayed for no reason. this led to tensions and riots, culminating in the burning of several migrant buildings and the decision by the authorities to declare lampedusa a port of no safety for migrants - not for fishermen and not for tourists, but for migrants. we now need to have detailed information about what really happened and i appeal to the italian authorities and the government to come back to parliament with this important information and to declare lampedusa a safe port immediately for all those people who need a safe port after having travelled in the mediterranean. because we cannot accept that more people's lives are at stake. we cannot accept deaths in the mediterranean while we're sitting here discussing it. now we must ensure that all member states take their responsibility and guarantee the safety of people's lives. i would like to thank you for your time and to reiterate that parliament is taking its responsibility and that it is now italy's turn to take its responsibility.
the rapporteur. - mr president! - what is it? i would like to begin by thanking my former colleague, diana wallis, who worked on this issue until she left parliament and i took over. it has been a very interesting and educational process to get into this issue and i would like to begin by thanking all the shadow rapporteurs for their constructive cooperation and willingness to really tackle this problem, and for all their contributions, comments and amendments. we are now there and we have a solution that we can all support and we voted unanimously in committee on this. why is it so important? yes, because international dialects are a reality throughout our member states. today we celebrate europe day and we rejoice in a europe that is growing ever closer together. our citizens move between countries, they have friends, acquaintances and family members from other member states. the integration of europe is something we all warmly welcome. but in keeping with the spirit of the times in which we find ourselves, more and more media outlets are willing to report on people and events in other countries. media is also consumed by people who live outside the country in which they were born and where they live and whose language they speak. it is therefore not unreasonable to assume that the number of civil actions involving parties in different member states may increase, and even increase to a considerable extent, and when this happens, a dispute arises as to which country's legislation should apply. to ensure that these international legal processes are handled in the best possible way, an amendment to the current rome ii regulation is needed and that is why we are discussing and debating this today. to this is added the possibility of so-called forum shopping, where parties try to direct their lawsuits to a particular country which has a particularly favourable legislation from their point of view, and this is of course not good. we're trying to limit this forum shopping in every jurisdiction. when the rome ii regulation was last negotiated in 2005, there was broad agreement in parliament on how these issues should be dealt with. unfortunately, the council was not able to reach a complete solution at that time, and the question has since then been hanging in the air and unresolved. we now feel that it is high time that we clarified this matter so that all concerned know what is going on. in the course of my work, predictability has been an unconditional requirement for me, because it is an absolute foundation of a modern legal system and a society of law. you gotta know what's going on. in the committee on legal affairs we have therefore taken parliament's 2005 position as a basis and made a number of necessary modifications. we now establish a clear basic rule that it must be the country where the plaintiff is domiciled and where a newspaper, tv channel or radio station or the like is targeted with its language and that is the country whose laws are to be used. if this is difficult to determine, the country of the seat of the editorial board shall be used. it is not reasonable for our journalists and responsible publishers to know the language laws of all countries before deciding whether to publish, adopting this can lead to self-censorship. everyone should know the rules of the game and the laws that apply, and it is important that this principle be upheld. within the framework of our common rules on fundamental rights and freedoms, it is then up to each member state to find the right balance between these rights. i hope now that the commission is prepared to take up the legislative proposals we have put forward and i hope we will be back shortly. i look forward to and welcome a message perhaps even during our discussion here tonight. thank you very much for this, mr president!
the rapporteur. - mr president! - what is it? my colleagues and dear commissioner. thank you for an interesting but brief discussion here in plenary. given the drama that has surrounded this report over the years, i would have expected a much more lively debate, but as i said, the report was adopted unanimously in committee and i really hope that it will also receive a great deal of support tomorrow. it is, as someone said, a matter of bridging the gap and finding the right balance between, on the one hand, freedom of the media and a fundamental freedom of expression and, on the other, the right of the individual to privacy and the rule of law. i welcome the commission's interest and i expect you, mr de gucht, to speak to viviane reding about this. i look forward to her returning to parliament with concrete proposals very soon, and there are some, so it should not take much work on the part of the commission to get us back to a proposal that we can certainly adopt. i trust this and look forward to the next proposal. it is good for our citizens to be able to say on this european day that we now want to fill the vacuum that has existed so that the free movement of persons and the legal security of citizens can be exploited again also from this point of view. i would like to thank you for your time and look forward to the vote in plenary tomorrow. thank you, thank you!

rapporteur. − madam president, i will speak english. normally i speak swedish in plenary, but i did all the rest of the work in english and when i tried to prepare the swedish, i realised that some of the words would be easier – this once – in english, because i had used them during the whole process. after an extensive process in committee, the report on discharge for the commission for the financial year 2010 has now arrived in plenary. first of all, i would like to express my gratitude to both the european court of auditors and the european commission for their very fruitful and professional cooperation during this process. i would also like to take this opportunity to thank the opinion-giving committees for their input. discharge is one of the main prerogatives of the european parliament, and it is important that we all try to take part in it in one way or another. let me turn to the most important question of any discharge procedure: whether or not to grant discharge. as you can see in the report adopted in the committee, which followed my recommendation to grant discharge to the commission for the financial year 2010, this was not an easy decision. we know that this is the 17th year that the commission has not received a clean bill from the court, and we can even see some small increases in error rates in certain areas. the reason we came to conclusion we did is that we got adequate responses from the commission to the four priority actions that the commission needs to address in the upcoming months. specifically, the following four points for action will be addressed by the commission and i hope it will confirm this today: firstly, financial engineering instruments; secondly, the commission’s accountability instruments; thirdly, pre-financing; and, fourthly, the commission’s supervisory role in the cohesion area. we want action in all of these areas in exchange for discharge. the financial engineering instruments kept us busy for most of the time. the court of auditors found severe weaknesses in the implementation of these instruments, especially in the area of cohesion. as you all are aware, the commission proposed using those instruments even more often in the future, which is why we invested a lot of effort in thinking about how we could correct the weaknesses detected. the commission is now being asked, in particular, to objectively and critically evaluate the use of those instruments and report back, and to assess the specific risks attached to the use of these instruments. some people make it sound like these instruments are some kind of alchemy that create gold out of nothing, but we see risk and we see costs, and we want them to be evaluated so that we know exactly what consequences they will have. the second point i mentioned concerns how the commission assumes its financial responsibility for implementing the eu budget. at the centre of that question is the annual activity report and the synthesis report. we need these to be improved and we need them to actually show more than they hide. as a result of our deliberations, the committee required that the commission deliver a political declaration in which it accepts its final and overall responsibility for the implementation of the budget, including the part which is implemented under shared management. we also need the commission to strengthen its annual activity report and for the signature of the commissioner responsible to be added next to that of the director-general, to show that the commission assumes responsibility. we also want the commission to analyse whether the commission’s own corporate governance arrangements could be further improved, for example by taking on some of the obligations it lays down on others in its green paper on eu corporate governance.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i shall focus solely on the report on the discharge of the european institute for gender equality (eige). as you all know, eige meets all the requirements of a sound economy and fulfils its commitments as mandated. unfortunately, paragraph 3 of the committee's proposal has been smuggled in with a sentence proposing a merger of the eige and the european union agency for fundamental rights (fra). this is a very strange proposal, considering that the eige was set up only a few years ago, after more than 10 years of pressure from this parliament. while there are of course points of contact with the fra, there are also many differences between them. eige focuses exclusively on gender and the structures and policies that can make us more effective in the area of gender equality. it is about the labour market, social security, decision-making and reconciling work and family life. the european parliament places gender equality high on the agenda even when member states sometimes lack vision and ideas. we therefore need a full-fledged institute that is a resource and carries out background guidance and follow-up in this important policy area. therefore, paragraph 3 should be deleted.
mr president, i would like to thank you. europe is facing serious economic and political problems. there is no excuse for the carelessness of public funds, and the error rate in the budget is unacceptably high. but we know that the commission is not responsible for all the mistakes it makes. the member states bear the main responsibility and the major shortcomings in the way the money has been spent at national level, as mentioned earlier, are unacceptable. the politicians in charge in these member states must answer for these mistakes - tory or not tory, labour or not labour. and it is not enough to point the finger at brussels and the eu when the negligence is in its own management. a number of member states have also refused to publish their annual reports on eu funds. why this filth? commissioner, you promised today that all reports would be made available. it's good and it's long overdue, but i think it's coming late. we should have more time to read these reports and make a thorough analysis. i therefore think that we should postpone the discharge decision for the commission so that we have time to go through all the facts. i think that is a reasonable demand, and in view of what my colleagues in my group have said before, i think that more people should be able to support it. finally, i would also like to comment on the discharge for the european development fund. the problem is that the edf is still outside the eu budget. the edf must become part of the normal budgetary control process.
president of the court of auditors. - mr president, i take this opportunity to thank the members of this house for their words of appreciation for the court of auditors and its work. the granting of discharge is a privilege of the european parliament. we have heard today calls for better supervision of work, better feedback, better financial management, greater simplification, stronger accountability mechanisms and transparency. i think it is also an opportunity for all the institutions to review the regulatory and implementation framework for the next programming period. we need clearer and simpler rules and objectives which really make sense in themselves, in order to create added value for the european union. the evolution of the union's economic governance also raises new questions, particularly as regards the accountability mechanism. the european stability mechanism is part of this development. i am pleased to announce that an audit committee of independent auditors has been set up and will report to parliament. i also heard calls for the court of auditors to submit its report on conflicts of interest in the agencies as soon as possible. i have already written to this institution several times: be assured that you will receive this report. but let us allow ourselves time to do it well and to conduct a contradictory procedure with the agencies concerned, so that it can bring real added value to your debates for other reasons.
member of the commission. − mr president, i would like to thank all the members who participated in this fruitful debate. your contributions demonstrate the will of the discharge authority to contribute to more effective budget execution, aiming not only to meet policy objectives but also to ensure that financial support reaches those who need it most. this is a question of effectiveness, fairness and justice. moreover, the european budget is the most concrete expression of the solidarity among european people and, therefore, it deserves the utmost care and commitment on the part of those managing and controlling it. improving the eu’s financial governance is a demanding task involving many stakeholders and financial actors. at the forefront are the citizens of europe and the benefits they derive from eu-financed projects and programmes. their interests and the protection of eu taxpayers’ money are the main drivers for our ambitions and our efforts to improve the financial management of the eu budget. next, we have the financial actors. the commission is fully committed to endorsing its final responsibility regarding implementation of the budget. but the effective involvement of all financial actors, without exception, and their commitment to value for money and sound financial management are instrumental to further reducing the risk of errors. in this respect, more effort is needed, in particular on the effectiveness of control and management systems, but also on the transparency and accountability of the european and national authorities in charge of ensuring optimal use of eu money. that is why the commission will endeavour to keep this parliament, as discharge authority, fully informed of the execution of the european budget. it will therefore continue to provide the necessary information in the accounts and via the various reports announced during the hearings of the commissioners responsible. moreover, the member states’ annual summaries, accompanied by an analysis and guidelines, will be transmitted in a user-friendly manner to the committee on budgetary control in accordance with the provisions of article 319 of the treaty. several concrete questions were raised during this debate, and i would like to reply to them. ms ivanova raised the issue of outstanding commitments, and i can only agree that the outstanding commitments need to be substantially reduced. therefore the commission has proposed an appropriate level of payments for the 2013 budget. then mr van de camp raised the issue of external funds in relation to the schengen information system (sis i and sis ii). you will know that many difficulties were encountered in the past in the development of this system. it is now operational and the commission is carefully monitoring it to ensure it functions in an effective and reliable manner. the commission is ready to report on it if requested to do so. with regard to the funds for external borders, the commission can execute the allocated budget when we receive claims from the member states. i will pass your concerns on to my fellow commissioner in charge of this matter. several of you, namely mr berman, ms hohlmeier, mr schmidt and ms jäätteenmäki, raised the issue of the status of the european development fund (edf). in the financial framework for 2014-2020 the commission has proposed keeping the same setting, i.e. outside the union budget, but it has also made a clear commitment to assess all other options for the financial period post-2020. there were, of course, many points made about the agencies, and i would like to say a few words regarding the food safety agency, in response to mr bové. the commission regrets the appointment of ms diána bánáti as an executive director of the international life of science institute, as that position is clearly incompatible with her role at the eu food safety agency. the commission will decide with the council on the next steps to be taken to replace ms bánáti. for the future, i want to inform you that the interinstitutional working group on traditional agencies, in which parliament is participating, has addressed the need to develop a consistent policy on preventing conflicts of interest for staff, advisers, experts and board members. this work has to be done in the working group and i hope it will produce an appropriate solution. to conclude, i want to assure you that the commission will address all other measures and actions included in the discharge resolution to be adopted today. many of these measures have already been proposed in the review of the financial regulation and in the legal bases for the next financial framework. the commission will also invite all the other parties actively concerned to join our efforts and to share our objective of further improving the execution of the eu budget.
president of the council. - mr president, i have to say something. i would like to thank the commissioner for his reply. i would like to conclude by saying that it has been exciting, interesting and important to listen to the many speeches by members of parliament. i hope that our two institutions can soon reach a viable agreement on a modernised discharge procedure. it is high time that we worked together to find a solution. as i mentioned in my introductory remarks, back in march 2011 the council put forward a proposal for a 'memorandum of understanding' for cooperation between parliament and the council during the annual discharge procedure. this memorandum contains concrete proposals on how we can exchange the necessary information and documents in a way that respects the respective roles of our institutions in the procedure. let me therefore state today that the council's proposal is not an ultimatum. it is clear that we must start negotiations between our institutions to reach an agreement, and i hope therefore that parliament will soon be able to set up a negotiating delegation so that we can get to work. we must all be aware of our responsibilities and do our part to find a solution. we owe this, not least to the european taxpayers, who are entitled to the fact that we must find together an efficient and sustainable way of controlling the expenditure of our institutions. mr president, ladies and gentlemen, i would like to thank you for your reply. this is not the time for institutional battles, but for cooperation and for showing our citizens that we can compromise and deliver. i hope therefore that we will be able to sit down together at the negotiating table very soon.
rapporteur. − mr president, i am sorry that i could not be here for the whole debate. i am very happy that the danish presidency is here – they are in other places as well having trialogues – but i have followed the debate extensively. the problem here is shared management, and shared management is exactly the same as it was when i was a kid and my sister and i had the shared responsibility of cleaning the house. nobody cleaned the house and then we stood there blaming each other. the problem is not all over the place. it is in structural funds and in all member states. 60% of the errors come from three member states: 31% from spain, 18% from italy and 10% from the uk. 58% of the errors could have been corrected by member states, according to the court of auditors. i think it is high time that the council debates this amongst themselves; it is your taxpayers, it is your money. that does not mean that the commission is without responsibility. they have the responsibility to control and sanction those who do not play by the rules. i ask myself: does the commission not have the tools to do that? or do you not use the tools that you have? my response has been that it is a combination. you lack some tools, but you do not use the tools which you have effectively enough. therefore, we have a responsibility in the upcoming programming period to give you the tools which you need now when we are co-legislators. that goes for the council as well. but we demand of you, as a part of this discharge procedure, to use the tools you have more effectively. the reason that i decided to propose to grant discharge is because i heard your sincere wish to change the way you work with sanctions, suspensions and interruptions and that is the reason we give you discharge. if, next year, we do not see any change, then it is a whole new deal. however, i think that we are on the right track and i am happy that you have been listening to the recommendations we have given in our resolution. i think that it is a good basis for improving the situation for next year.
in writing. − the european parliament recently voted in favour of an own-initiative report on the future of regional airports and air services in the eu (2011/2196 (ini)). i personally voted against the report. the report highlighted the many positive aspects of regional airports. however, instead of helping these regional airports, which are often served by low-cost carriers, measures were proposed that severely restrict the competitiveness of these carriers. a number of regulations on hand luggage, charges for voluntary payment by credit card, the possibility of employing staff through staffing companies, are cutting off the legs of many low-cost companies (paragraphs 42-45 of the report).
in writing. − for the first time since 2006, the annual report of the european court of auditors showed an increase in the proportion of errors committed in the implementation of the eu budget from 3,3% (in 2009) to 3,7%. this increase is extremely worrying and must not be allowed to recur. the european commission is ultimately responsible for the use of eu money. however, the court's criticism is not directed primarily at the commission but at the member states. for about 80% of eu expenditure, the management of the money is shared between the commission and the member states, and the member states are responsible for the payments. it is also in these areas of expenditure that the error rate increased most in 2010. we therefore believe that the commission should be given a discharge and that the member states should instead discuss in the council measures to correct these errors.
in writing. − we consider it unacceptable that not all member states' annual summaries were made available as part of the budgetary control process for the commission's 2010 budget. it is contrary to the existing interinstitutional agreement and does not contribute to an open and transparent eu or to the enhancement of trust in the union's institutions among eu citizens. that is why we had originally intended to vote in favour of postponing the discharge decision for the commission's 2010 budget. the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a directive on the protection of the environment against the risks arising from the use of certain substances in the production of certain products. we welcome the announcement but at the same time we want to stress that this is a decision that should have come much earlier in the process. the promised publication comes too late for us to support a discharge for the commission. we therefore choose to abstain from voting on this item.
in writing. - when the eu demands budgetary restraint and thrift from the member states, it is not credible to allow millions of euros to go to the moving carousel between brussels and strasbourg. that is why i voted for the discharge for the implementation of parliament's 2010 budget and for the resolution in general, but also for the texts which point out the unreasonableness of the situation. it is also important to point out that in the case of the moving carousel, it is not parliament that is blocking. the decision rests with the council and the representatives of the individual member states.
the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' in the definition of the term 'competent authority'. it is therefore of the utmost importance that the eu authorities and their activities are scrutinised by the european court of auditors and by the european parliament through the discharge process. however, we believe that the rapporteur's approach is too critical and too focused on details rather than on the overall and important issues of implementing the decisions taken at european level. we support the authorities as they can help us to make our food safer, our environment cleaner and our planes and flights safer. the rapporteur's criticism also concerns in large part situations which have already been addressed and situations which the authorities themselves have no control over. it is important to safeguard the independence of the authorities, but when the authority cannot do more than what has already been done in individual cases, it is wrong to take these as revenue for not granting a discharge. we consider that the correction and follow-up of any errors is sufficient and that the authorities should therefore be granted a discharge.
in writing. − one of the areas where eu cooperation has the potential to create clear added value for the member states and all their citizens is research cooperation. expensive and resource-intensive projects that are difficult and costly for individual states to implement become cheaper for the member state and feasible if cooperation is at european level. that is why i voted in favour of the resolution which gives discharge to the implementation, financial management and control of the eu authorities in general, but i opposed the proposals which seek to reduce or cut specifically research in european cooperation.
in writing. the rapporteur has done a good job of highlighting important aspects of the work and governance of the european medicines agency (ema) and the european environment agency (eea) respectively. however, as regards the rapporteur's criticism of possible conflicts of interest, we consider that the ema and the eea have provided adequate answers to the european parliament's questions. we therefore support the liberal group's proposal to grant discharge to both of these european authorities.
in writing. − the margin of error for the use of eu funds in the cohesion fund and the energy/transport funds increased by 7.7% in 2010. this was largely due to shortcomings in the public procurement procedure. common funds that are not used for their intended purpose are a problem. in the management of common funds, the eu institutions themselves must set a good example, especially today, in the light of what economic mismanagement has led to in large parts of europe. that is why i voted in favour of postponing the discharge for the implementation of the budget for the european environment agency for 2010. the rapporteur has pointed out a number of points where there was a breach: conflict of interest of the executive director which may have significantly affected the work of the agency, incorrect procurement of services and misuse of eu funds. it is also pointed out that this is a suspension of liability. the agency has demonstrated its responsiveness on many points and the quality of the supporting research reports produced is good. therefore, an extended deadline is what is needed to correct irregularities and explain oneself to the european parliament.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' in the context of the directive. we do not believe that price regulation at the consumer level solves the structural problems of the roaming market, as regulation prevents prices from falling below the regulated price. we therefore welcome the fact that the new regulation contains market mechanisms that can increase competition in the roaming market and thus create lower prices for consumers. in particular, we welcome the proposal for a so-called 'local break out', which would enable consumers to connect directly via local networks when abroad, without having to use the data room of their home provider.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'competent authority' in the context of the directive. we do not believe that price regulation at the consumer level solves the structural problems of the roaming market, as such regulation prevents prices from falling below the regulated price. we therefore welcome the fact that the new regulation contains market mechanisms that can increase competition in the roaming market and thus create lower prices for consumers. in particular, we welcome the proposal for a local break-out, which would enable consumers to connect directly via local networks when abroad, without having to use the data room of their home provider.
in writing. - i voted for the report. the report goes further than the commission and proposes a lower price ceiling for roaming calls. it is a step in the right direction, although i personally think the ceiling could be even lower. the report also proposes some improvements in transparency and consumer protection. here too, far more could be done to safeguard the interests of consumers, but since it is, after all, a step in the right direction, i am voting in favour of the report.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environment from harmful substances. we believe that the conflict of laws rules in this area should not be allowed to conflict with the swedish freedom of the press regulation and the freedom of expression act. the proposal presented may lead to such conflicts when swedish courts are to apply foreign law. if this means that the current exemption does not remain, legislation should very clearly give precedence to constitutional provisions in the individual country such as the swedish freedom of the press ordinance and the freedom of expression act.
for the gue/ngl group. president. - the debate is closed. i am not going to sing the praises of the single market but rather focus on its shortcomings. the eu's neoliberal economic policy, emu, and the single market today place the principle of the free movement of goods ahead of the interests of citizens. it has created a new europe a europe of mass unemployment, poverty and growing class divisions, while pushing back the drive for a better environment and gender equality. the report refers to the krakow declaration, which describes the single market as 'the most powerful tool for restoring sustainable growth'. however, the single market cannot in itself create sustainable growth and more jobs. it's not possible. because it requires a whole new economic policy that is green and expansive, makes investments in the public sector and puts people before the system. paragraph 33 states that the eu is to be a direct human contact with 'citizens and consumers through the commission representation office'. that's a great idea! actively invite citizens, let men and women pour into the representative offices to share their views on the single market and the current austerity policies - policies that have devastated the lives and futures of millions of people. the basic principle and objective of the internal market should be the good of people and the environment, not the interests of big business as is the case today. the present single market has also erected a high and guarded wall against the outside world. the single market scoreboard says nothing about the thousands of poor people from the south who hoped for a better future in europe but drowned in the high waves of the mediterranean.
mr president, i would like to thank you. let me begin by saying that ukraine is europe and has its place in europe. eu-ukraine cooperation, such as the association agreement, is also important for ukraine and its european ambitions. to get closer to the eu, or to become part of it one day, you must share the same values as the eu. this applies to all countries that want a closer relationship with the eu, including ukraine, regardless of who is currently in power. one of the first things you notice when these values diverge from the eu's is that human rights and the rule of law are not working. in the case of ukraine, we in the european parliament have been concerned for a long time about both of these things, above all because we have not been able to see that the ukrainian state makes a distinction between political trials and legal trials. criminal acts must be punished for their criminal character, but political trials must cease. if ukraine is serious about moving closer to the eu, legal proceedings must be transparent and in line with eu standards. human rights must be respected and the media must be independent. without these three things, we will have a more closed society, and this is not the way we want to see ukraine go. the eu and the european parliament have always been clear about what we expect from our partners. it is now up to the people of ukraine to demand these changes. we cannot deviate from our values, but we can help ukraine transform into a modern democracy and we are ready to do so.
in writing. - we swedish social democrats voted in favour of the report on the european union's internal security strategy because we support its main message: that the security strategy should focus on those areas where eu cooperation brings a clear added value, while respecting subsidiarity. paragraph 19 deals with the development of a european legal culture. we agree that mutual trust between member states in the field of justice should be strengthened. however, we believe that this development should be achieved primarily by strengthening fundamental and procedural rights in the member states to a uniform level and not by creating a complete european legal culture. we do not support increased links between the common security and defence policy (csdp) and justice and home affairs (jha) work, nor do we support increased cooperation between the eu and nato.
in writing. - i voted against the report. the main reason for this is that the report emphasises the importance of federal development in the field of internal security, with europol and eurojust as some of the tools. i also believe that the citizens' privacy in the area of information security is not sufficiently safeguarded in this report.
in writing. - we voted in favour of the own-initiative report on a strategy to strengthen the rights of vulnerable consumers. we welcome both the fact that the report emphasizes subsidiarity and that it does not seek to establish different levels of consumer protection, but that the starting point is a high level of consumer protection for all. we believe that vulnerable consumers should be given the same opportunity to make free and informed choices. we therefore support self-regulatory initiatives and increased cooperation between the commission, the member states and industry to strengthen consumer empowerment. we also welcome the fact that the report highlights the importance of responsible advertising aimed at children on tv and the internet, since children do not always have the ability to understand and judge messages. we support the idea that advertising should not be regulated at eu level, but that it is essential that businesses implement the voluntary initiatives and codes of conduct that have been developed.

in writing. - strong consumer protection for all consumers active on the european market is essential. the key is that consumers have access to simple and reliable information about services and goods. the consumers we need to give greater protection to are those who cannot take advantage of the information available. a too broad definition of who is a 'vulnerable consumer' risks diluting the protection of those individuals who really need special support. for us, with a liberal mindset, the concept of vulnerability should be based on the needs of the individual, not group affiliation. we did not hear that view during the course of the debate, but we are not voting against the report, because it raises an important issue which we believe needs further discussion.
in writing. - the importance of basing the debate on the situation of women in turkey on facts and of recognising the progress made; however, it is not for the eu to recommend how turkey should promote gender equality, whether turkey should introduce a quota system, or priorities in education and family policy. i welcome the steps turkey has taken to promote women's rights and participation, particularly in education and the labour market. what we can do is to continue to work together to promote female entrepreneurship, not only in the cities but also in the countryside. it is important that we show respect for the country's culture, traditions and religion and that we do not impose our model on turkey. at the same time, traditions must not be an excuse for the oppression of women. i regularly stress the importance of dialogue with the turkish government and members of parliament on the alarming number of honour killings and violence against women in the home. it is very positive that turkey has ratified the convention on preventing and combating violence against women and has recently adopted laws and action plans. they are aware that the challenge is also to change the attitudes of society and the perception of the role of women, and to ensure that changes are implemented in practice.
in writing. - we chose to abstain from today's vote on the report 'a 2020 perspective for women in turkey'. turkey is not a member of the eu, and therefore we should not call on turkey to change its domestic policy.
in writing. - i voted for the report. this is because the report calls for women's rights and gender equality issues to be at the heart of the ongoing negotiations on turkey's possible accession to the european union. it is particularly positive that my amendments, which highlight the particular vulnerability of kurdish women in turkish society, have been included in the report. i believe, however, that much stronger criticism and international condemnation of the systematic repression of the kurds by the turkish state is needed. the rapporteur should also have paid attention to the fact that there are currently 500 kurdish women in prison and expressed his dissatisfaction. but since the report is still moving in the right direction in its recommendations on the human rights and equal rights of all women in turkey, i have decided to vote in favour of the report.

madam president, i would like to thank you. for me, the rights of lgbt people are much more important to talk about in the european parliament than milk quotas, because of astrid lulling's agenda item yesterday. simon busuttil, we have to get emotional! every day, people in our union are abused simply because of who they love or who they are. they are oppressed, discriminated against, abused, or even killed. it is tragic that today, in 2012, we in europe have not come any further on this issue. we have an important discrimination directive lying in the council's desk because there are contradictions within the council on the rights of lgbt people. in hungary, a new constitution stipulates that marriage is between a man and a woman. in lithuania and latvia, there is a bill that says that information about homosexuality should be banned. one of the easiest words to understand and one of the most beautiful words there is is the word family. the eu offers free movement for citizens, including families, but not for everyone. the eu definition of family is out of step with reality. this is deeply regrettable, because one of the pillars of eu cooperation is human rights, and that includes the rights of lgbt people. we have to sweep in front of our own door.
mr president, i would like to thank you. this is an unusually balanced report on the balance of trade with china. this is not the usual tail-singing that i think we usually hear when the eu talks about trade with china. my dad always says that whoever gets in the way of progress will only get run over. world trade and the world economy look very different today than they did when i was growing up. it's going to look even more different in 10, 15, 20 years. it's a fact, and there's nothing to stand in the way of that. the fact that china has grown so much in the international trade arena is basically a good thing, not only for the human aspects with all the poverty eradicated, but also something that we have been able to benefit from with increased exports and increased imports of cheap products, low inflation and so on. basically, this is because of comparative advantages from china, differences that are what drive trade. however, this does not mean that china always plays by the rules. on the contrary, china abuses many rules and we should be particularly tough. they have a lousy respect for intellectual property. we'll fight about that. they have a lousy policy on investment opportunities in china, not to mention the protectionism they apply online with the great firewall. we must fight all of this, but we must not fight chinese exports. after all, the eu is the biggest exporter to china. we're profiting from trade with china. the most important thing we can do if we want to get to the balance of trade with china is to work on our own competitiveness. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and it is hoping that the council will take them up. i think there are opportunities with trade with china. i am therefore glad that this report points to more than just problems.
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the discussions on the agreements with colombia and peru show that human rights are involved. i want to raise the issue of freedom of speech, the right to know and the right to education. the agreement negotiated by the eu with colombia and peru, together with several other agreements imposed on these countries, contains strong intellectual property rights obligations and inflexible trade rules without ensuring corresponding limitations on the monopoly positions these rights imply. in colombia, this has led to an act such as making a private copy or a limited copy for educational purposes now being punishable, criminal. we need to give importance to our trade relations with third countries, to freedom of expression, to the right to education and to the right to knowledge. we need agreements that introduce and strengthen copyright restrictions at the international level. there is no good reason why the union should not lead such a development at international level. nor are there any good reasons for us to approve an agreement which does not take steps towards a better policy in this area.
mr president, honourable members, the minister and the commissioner! it is no wonder that many citizens in europe are outraged at the banks' compliance with the requirements of moderation and reason in terms of remuneration and risk-taking. a transaction tax, however, is not the answer to public anger. it is a bad tax which risks increasing the costs of ordinary bank customers, hindering investment in europe, damaging growth and thereby putting jobs at risk. i also believe that the calculation of the revenue is uncertain. for 40 years this proposal has been discussed, and it has been introduced once in europe, in my home country of sweden. as a result, the bulk of stock trading moved from stockholm to london. if a transaction tax is to be introduced, it must be introduced globally or at least throughout the eu. however, i think the financial sector should contribute more tax revenue. a better model is, for example, to introduce a stability levy for all banks. to summarize very briefly: i think the banking sector and the financial sector should pay taxes. it's not a problem for me, but this is a bad tax that will be counterproductive. mr commissioner, try again, make a new effort. blue-card answer. – yes, mrs mccarthy, but you do not know how that is going to work in practice. you do not know that because it is open to question; you may argue, but it is open to question. there are too many questions here. the minister said that in her introductory remarks. why cannot we be sure whether this is a good tax or is a counterproductive tax? mrs mccarthy, i think i pay more taxes than you have done. i am prepared to pay more taxes but they have to be fair and they have to be good taxes. this is a bad solution.
mr president, there are a number of reasons for parliament to vote against the financial transaction tax. firstly, sweden tried this in the 1980s and it failed. it undermined swedish financial markets and the city of london gained. it also caused more speculation, and all the experience shows that it did not help. also, this was at a time when the financial markets were less global and less mobile. now, the opposite is true. secondly, even the commission recognises that the financial transaction tax will lead to less growth. the discussion is about how much less growth, but it will give less growth, which is contrary to what europe needs today. we need more growth and we need reforms that support economic growth and dynamic financial markets in europe. thirdly, the financial transaction tax is not providing us with new money. it will be paid by customers – those who are going to invest and those who trust in their future pension schemes. it will be paid by all of us, because there are no taxes paid by anyone apart from the citizens of europe. this tax will not help in the europe of today, so we should vote ‘no’.
in writing. - i voted for the report. this is an improved recovery plan for tuna compared to the previous one. it allows smaller catches and is based on iccat recommendations. of course, it would be better if no catches were allowed at all, but i felt that this was still a step towards a more sustainable fishery.
in writing. - the financial sector is undertaxed. it has a tax advantage of being exempt from vat, which according to the european commission corresponds to about 0.11-0.17 percent of gdp. the result is an unfair distribution of resources. risk capital that should be allocated to other sectors is instead used in a less socially beneficial way by financial firms. there are still many questions about this tax, but it is nevertheless reasonable to see it as one of the options that should be included in the continuing debate on how to correct the undertaxation of the financial sector. if a transaction tax is to be introduced, the clear direction must be that it is to be introduced at a global level. the possibility of the eu going ahead should not be dismissed, but this should be seen as a first step towards a global tax. what we have decided today is not a european tax, but a framework for taxation of transactions where taxation remains entirely at the national level. we cannot afford to transfer taxation powers to the eu. today's vote was essentially about the transaction tax as such, not about how the national revenues should be used. the revenue aspects are dealt with separately in other policy processes.'
in writing. - i voted for the report. it is important to introduce a tax on financial transactions. the deregulation of financial markets is one of the main causes of the global economic crisis. i think the report could have gone further, both in terms of scope and the level of the tax rates. i would also like to see clearer wording on the use of the proceeds for aid, environmental and social projects in developing countries. i would also like to see additional tax rates in the event of rapid and increasing movements of financial transactions through the use of automatic tax rates linked to the pace of financial movements. but since the introduction of this tax is nevertheless a step in the right direction, i voted in favour. finally, i would like to stress that, contrary to what is stated in the report, i do not believe that tax revenues should go to the eu budget, but should be distributed among the member states.
in writing. - the proposal for a regulation on the european parliament and council directive on the approximation of the laws of the member states relating to the labelling, presentation and advertising of foodstuffs for sale to the ultimate consumer; it is more than astonishing that a majority of meps have confidence in a 'financial transaction tax', as advocated in the report on the introduction of a financial transaction tax (ftt). attitudes have changed dramatically in the past year among party groups and members. the idea that a ftt would provide funds to solve virtually all economic problems has taken hold and seems almost parodyary. it is important to recall that a tobin tax was tried in sweden and proved to be a waste. transactions are moved from sweden. it is also a matter of course to stress that the cost of ftt is borne by the general consumer and that economic growth is being hampered.

the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy has tabled a number of amendments to the proposal. - mr president! - what is it? i would like to begin by thanking gerben-jan gerbrandy for a good report. as rapporteur for the committee on industry on this matter, i would like to stress that the promotion of sustainable consumption of resources is not a contradiction but rather a prerequisite for strengthening the industrial competitiveness of the eu. we need innovative business models, research and innovation, a reuse market, an extended ecodesign directive and a faster implementation of the framework directive on waste. in our opinion from the committee on industry, we also raised the question of the need to develop and strengthen the forms of interaction between the member states and the eu. in our view, this can be done with clear, measurable and verifiable objectives, indicators and measures, including specific objectives and concrete legislative initiatives, supported by the necessary funding mechanisms. this is an important question in this context too.
mr president, i would like to thank you. resource efficiency is really about getting better at doing more with less. it also opens up new jobs and new growth, i.e. long-term sustainable growth that is climate and environmentally compatible and which is therefore becoming an important factor in competition. this report focuses on investment in innovation and research. the report also states, for example, that subsidies to environmentally harmful activities must be stopped. it's very good, and it's not a day too early. it is a good basis for change of course, it is a good basis for new investment and for new jobs. in the work on the european parliament's raw materials strategy, it was also clear that resource efficiency is directly linked to security of supply. europe's industry depends on metals, minerals and other raw materials. when we develop ideas like 'urban mining', we also develop new industries. but above all we are helping to ensure that existing industry also has access to the raw materials it needs. it's about saving resources. it is the wise use of resources. this report is therefore an important contribution to further discussion and action. at the same time, the implications of the tax shift, i.e. the redistribution of tax from labour to the environment, require a more in-depth debate.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i would like to thank you, edith bauer, for helping to tackle the large pay gap between women and men with your report. of course, a lot of different measures are needed to deal with this. i'm just going to give you one perspective. the basis for equal pay is in fact a well-developed public sector with good social infrastructure, such as childcare and homes for the elderly, so that women can establish themselves much more easily in the labour market and have the same opportunities for full-time work as men. shared parental leave is another important measure, because we know that women stay at home with children to a much greater extent than men. this contributes to employers' perception of women as a more insecure workforce, which naturally leads to a deterioration in their wage trends and working conditions. to stop wage discrimination, we must therefore stop cuts and privatisations in the public sector and allow men to take more of the parental leave.
the commission is not prepared to accept this. the most important thing in all wage formation is that we have free and strong trade unions, which are not hindered by the posting directives or other eu regulations. at the same time, it is political values and decisions that determine the social climate in which wages are negotiated. it is important that we have had a principle in the eu member states for 50 years, namely equal pay for equal work. the pay gap between men and women, for example, is still 16 to 17 per cent. in some places, the pay gap between women and men has even widened. it could be said that this means that the directive on equal treatment for women and men has been turned into a paper tiger. all agree that the unfair pay gap between women and men should be tackled, but there are very few concrete measures. it may have to do with the complexity of the causes. discrimination involves a variety of factors, social factors, undervaluing of women's work, inequality in work-life balance, traditions and stereotypes. on this basis, the member states of the eu must invest in social welfare, invest in childcare, care for the elderly and parental leave covering both mothers and fathers. in this context, the eu could help to push forward.
madam president, i would like to thank you. there are more women than men in europe today. yet we are not seen as equally worthy. how is that possible in 2012? how is it that we are standing here again asking for something that was written into the treaty more than 40 years ago? why is it that a man working in a typical woman's job often gets paid more than a woman, while a woman working in a typical man's job gets paid less than a man? why is it that member states do not succeed in achieving equal pay for equal work, despite gender equality laws? my answer to these questions may be quite controversial it depends on the man. men in positions of power have to put their foot down. the men must take responsibility and share the payroll. men have to share the responsibility of the home and the children. look around you, how many men are in this debate? we will never be equal unless we fight together, side by side, men and women. mr president, i would like to thank you. japan is the third largest economy in the world and a very important trading partner for us here in europe. but trade between us is not uncomplicated. after meeting with many european and japanese companies, i think the picture is quite clear. the major challenges facing european companies in japan are discriminatory bureaucracy and hassle and a lot of protectionist rules. japanese companies, for their part, complain all the more about european tariffs. i am therefore glad that we are now slowly but surely approaching the opening of free trade negotiations, because it is precisely with this tool that both these problems are being tackled. and now that the discussion on the scope is over, we can also see clearly what benefits we can expect from a free trade agreement. it is therefore important that we now take the next step, that the council establishes a clear mandate for negotiations and that the commission starts negotiations. as we have heard in part here in the house today, there is some resistance to these negotiations, but i think it is a little exciting because this resistance looks a little different from what it usually does when we discuss free trade negotiations. it is not the usual one, the one about agricultural protectionism or the talk about social and environmental dumping and it is not north against south, but here it feels more difficult because here it is often economies that are similar to each other that are to be met. it's about equal rules and i don't think this is going to be an easy negotiation. a number of us who visited japan to discuss with japanese representatives the conditions for a free trade agreement, and i must admit that i was not fully convinced of their determination and within the framework of negotiations that we can put the pressure we need to open up the japanese market to european companies. we need a free trade agreement. in these times when it is more popular than ever to talk about reforms for growth, there is no other growth reform that can be as good and effective as a free trade agreement. it is a reform that does not cost money and unfortunately it seems that there are many european leaders and many representatives who do not recognize such a reform despite staring it in the face. i therefore propose that we propose as soon as possible to enter into full negotiations with japan on a free trade agreement.
the rapporteur. - mr president! - what is it? so on wednesday we shall be voting on the generalized system of preferences (gsp), as we usually abbreviate it. as rapporteur, i would like to take this opportunity to begin by thanking the commission, the danish presidency and, in particular, all the shadow rapporteurs with whom i have had the privilege of working on this matter. we have had a very good cooperation and i think that is also apparent when we look at the results. the gsp is the eu's most important trade policy tool, especially in trade with developing countries, and no other tool covers so many countries. and with the help of the gsp, we can help developing countries in their efforts to achieve basic human rights, to reduce poverty and to achieve sustainable development in general. and we do this by lowering or eliminating tariffs on their goods. this is the first time that the european parliament has been involved in legislating on the gsp, despite the fact that it has been in existence for more than 40 years, and i would like to start by saying that i think the fact that we can stand here in the plenary today and discuss a proposal with an agreement in the baggage one year after the proposal was presented is a success in itself. early on in this work i met many people who thought that things would be more complicated and difficult now that the european parliament was involved in decision-making and perhaps it has become a little more complicated, but no one can say anything but that we are working purposefully and effectively. we have put pressure on the council to come to a decision and instead of following their lead, they have had to take a stand based on our standpoints. the new proposal and the one we have now negotiated with the danish presidency means that those countries which have managed to make economic progress or which have other more favourable agreements with the eu are no longer included in the generalized system of preferences and are not allowed to take part in this particular system. i'll be honest with you, when i started working on this issue and i looked at some of the countries that were considered developing countries, i found it difficult to understand how some of the gulf states, for example, could be considered developing countries. however, we are also very clear that we are safeguarding and maintaining that part of the system of generalized preferences which ensures that the poorest countries in the world do not have to pay any customs duties on any products, except arms, to europe. it is perhaps the single aspect of the eu's trade policy that i think we have the most reason to be proud of. our work in the european parliament has been aimed at making the gsp system transparent, predictable and more generous for those who remain in the system. the most important debate and the most difficult debate i think we had was about the criteria for selection. we knew that we needed stable and internationally recognised criteria that could stand up to a wto review and so we concluded that the $4 000 originally proposed by the commission was probably the most reasonable. however, we have made some adjustments in the transitional period. to make the system more generous to those who remain in it, we have also included more products that can be of value to poor countries and increase their ability to trade for prosperity. we have also strengthened the requirements for joining the eu's flagship human rights scheme, gsp+, while making it more attractive to join. i just want to mention something about gsp+, because it's the eu's system of giving additional trade preferences in exchange for countries committing, committing to live up to 27 different conventions for human rights and for sustainable development. i would like to conclude by thanking all those who have been involved in this work and i would like to say that it will be many years, 10 years more, before we have to discuss this issue again. i hope that in 10 years' time we will have even fewer countries in the gsp - partly because many have concluded even more favourable agreements with the eu, but above all because we have fewer poor developing countries as a consequence and of the trade for development we are doing here.
madam president, i would like to thank you. i am more concerned about those who cannot afford to buy any clothes than i am about the european textile industry, and given that these countries account for only 4% of our imports, i think the most generous terms should apply. so i worry that we're removing the vulnerability criteria that have existed in the past. if a country has no diversified industry, then perhaps a good way to try to help create it is to make sure that they can export here without tariffs. i am also concerned that we are creating artificial borders within regional free trade areas or cooperation areas where the majority of countries are poor. we should also be able to continue to treat them as gsp+ countries. i think that this agreement does not help these countries enough and that is a disadvantage when you go too fast in the first reading agreements. many of these countries have not had a chance to make their voices heard in a good way and many of those affected have not been able to make their voices heard. we make too many first-instance deals, and this is yet another example of that.
member of the commission. − madam president, one of the amendments put forward raises the problem that if one country in a customs union is a gsp country, and the others are not, this would create imbalances. i can hardly imagine the opposite being the case: would you, for example, agree to china obtaining ‘everything but arms’ status as soon as it entered into an agreement with, say, laos or cambodia, to set up a customs union by a later date, for example within 25 years? it would be very difficult to imagine that being the right answer to the question. you could say the same of india, pakistan, thailand or malaysia, which do the same with ldcs. needless to say, this would accelerate preference erosion, with a significant drop in ldc exports. they would be squeezed completely out of the market with such a system. several members have asked why we did not include other criteria. we have given a lot of thought to this issue, but our conclusion is that we have to ensure wto compatibility, and the use of a single indicator which is internationally recognised is, we think, the best solution. it would be a disservice to developing countries, and to the credentials of the eu as a serious partner, if we added more criteria and, as a result, lost yet another wto case on gsp. this would lead to a full redesign of the new scheme just after it entered into force. therefore, we should stick to an internationally recognised, objective way to differentiate among countries. another question that has been raised a couple of times relates to using the human development index as an indicator. again, we looked into the possibility of using the human development index in gsp, but this index is not well suited to this exercise. human development index data are not complete in terms of time or countries covered; also, human development index categories are relative and fixed. this means that countries may develop significantly, and the development needs which the gsp should address may change, but they will remain in the same category. for example, no mater how much china grows it is likely to stay in the same ‘medium’ category and thus, basically, keep gsp preferences forever. these problems are acknowledged by the united nations itself. they would render the human development index inadequate for the gsp and enhance the risk of wto challenge. the human development index is a good index to establish a long-term picture of social development, but it is ill-suited to the short-term analysis of export needs which the gsp requires. finally, as regards transition periods, i would like to say that these were the subject of negotiations between council, parliament and the commission up to a few weeks ago, and i am satisfied with the compromise achieved. a number of countries no longer receiving gsp benefits are in free trade negotiations with us. we look forward to their conclusion and recall the eu commitment to assist, pending wto membership, and to support development as per eu development policy – a debate we will come to later on. finally, i would like to thank all colleagues for their comments and i look forward to the vote on the gsp tomorrow.
the rapporteur. - madam president! - what is it? just a few quick comments. i am not surprised that there are some colleagues who feel that things have gone too fast. i am not taking it into account, however, because the result has been poor. on the contrary! we have a very large majority for this compromise here in the european parliament and we have been discussing this issue for several years. just because you disagree with everything does not mean that you can postpone the process and say we will negotiate until every member of this parliament is in favour of the compromise. we have a good compromise and i think we should guard it. the big question that has been discussed most here today has of course been what criteria should apply to whether or not a country should be included in the generalised system of preferences. we didn't want to have a situation where everyone chooses a criterion that suits their favorite country. it's easy to sit in a country and say that this is the country i want to be part of and that's why i choose this criterion. we have tried to find something that is clear, fair and above all that will stand up to the test of the wto. and we did. the part that i think most members here should be very proud of is the whole 'everything but arms' issue, which gives the world's poorest countries free access to the eu market. i've discussed this with american representatives and they say it's a brilliant idea and that they also want "everything but guns"... and sugar and cocoa and coffee and cotton and bananas. this is something that europe is doing and showing leadership, and i think we should continue to try to get the rest of the world to join us. i'm proud of the deal. it provides an efficient, predictable and transparent trading system. we didn't take the crisis into account to be less, to be more generous, but rather more generous to the poorest of the world. that's the result of this deal.
mr president, i would like to thank you. when we listen to jean-marie le pen, we understand the importance of acting quickly. we know what the isolationist, nationalist europe that jean-marie le pen wants has led to in european history and that is not the europe i want to live in. i welcome the current packages and legislative proposals and i believe they are necessary to get europe and euro-cooperation out of the crisis. we should also give the countries outside the euro area the opportunity to join in time - we agree, madam minister. the rules on budgetary control certainly need to be tightened up. i saw in the economist a ship that went down, and the headline was, when will mrs. merkel start the engines? yes, but it may not be so bad that this ship is floating before the engines start and therefore i believe that the measures we are taking now are essential to keep the boat floating and then it will be able to get going. this is our experience in sweden in the 1990s: it was not about tightening for the sake of tightening but about giving the opportunity to grow and i think this is a good proposal that gives europe that opportunity.
mr president, we are facing deeper and deeper problems that are threatening the stability and the welfare of our societies. if increased spending and increased deficits had been the solution to our problems we would have no problems today. the country reports presented by the commission speak a plain language: it is a matter of a failure to make reforms and of ongoing imbalances based upon too much spending. i say this because we cannot avoid the problem, it is at the core of europe and present in all of our societies. the solution is not inventing new institutions or inventing new funds for new spending, it is making reforms in all member states, something we have known for a number of years. we need to act together and we need to have common rules. what is even more important is making all the reforms at european level – we can decide on this today – and at member state level that can open up growth and new jobs. we all know what sort of reforms are needed. mr president, we do not need yet more new pacts; we need acts.
mr president, i would like to thank you. the report by the committee on economic and monetary affairs contains strong words in defence of trade union rights and freedoms. all measures under this legislation shall respect national wage formation and national collective agreements. these are important markings. the commission has committed a number of excesses during the crisis. extortion-like demands have reduced minimum wages, dismantled the collective bargaining structure and excluded trade unions from negotiations. the labour market has been particularly damaged in greece, ireland and portugal. these ugly habits must end. it is unacceptable that the rights and freedoms of the citizens are being violated. besides, attacking unions is counterproductive. only in constructive cooperation, where all parties are involved and take responsibility, can the labour market function effectively.

the internal market and its infrastructure, crucially needed in order to boost jobs, sustainable growth and competitiveness. i therefore very much welcome the broad agreement on my report on project bonds. in this situation we must avoid deep splits between institutions and member states, particularly between richer net payer countries and the poorer ones. in this situation we must build bridges, not dig trenches. let me focus on the negotiations on the financial framework. they are complicated, as several different procedures are involved. codecision on sector programmes, consent on the mff as such – meaning a veto for the european parliament – a right for parliament to be heard on own resources, and on top of that an interinstitutional agreement. this is not easy, so let us speak clearly. this parliament, elected to represent the citizens, will not accept that member states’ leaders take decisions in areas subject to codecision such as structural funds, research, agricultural infrastructure, etc., without a full codecision procedure. that procedure can be finalised only when we have an agreement on the financial envelopes. two, the positions on the mff agreed by the european council must be negotiated between parliament and the general affairs council in order to get our consent. a fait accompli will not be accepted. three, parliament and council should have fully-fledged negotiations on all mff-related aspects and programmes before deciding on figures and finalising the mff package. to conclude, i agree with mr wammen: nothing is agreed until everything is agreed, but that includes mff ceilings and legislative programmes as well as own resources. i am quite convinced that we will be able to find a reasonable solution as we know how important this is for the future of europe. we simply cannot afford to fail.
