blue-card answer. – no i would not, because i do not think that it is the euro that is the problem. it is the lack of policies to support the monetary union that is the problem. when we have those policies in place i would recommend my own country to join the euro as well. (applause)
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'commercial vehicle' to cover vehicles with a maximum mass exceeding 10 tonnes. but we do not think it is fair that gdp per capita should be the only criterion for a country to be covered by the gsp. we do not think that the world bank classification is sufficient, but the eu should also take into account other factors that show how vulnerable countries are, such as diversification, integration into world trade and ranking on the undp human development index, when making the classification. we support the fact that the countries covered by the 'everything but arms' (eba) targeting the approximately 50 countries that the un designates as the world's least developed countries are not affected by the gsp amendment.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'common carrier' in the definition of the term 'commercial vehicle'. the proposal contains many important tools to ensure budgetary discipline among euro area members, which has been underlined by developments in recent years. in the process, proposals for joint borrowing and an eu investment fund equivalent to 1% of gdp have been introduced, which would only lead to further debt accumulation. we have voted against these individual parts. the commission proposal, on the other hand, concerns instruments and frameworks which it is urgent to decide on, especially in the present situation. we have therefore voted in favour of the proposal in the other parts. we assume that the parts dealing with increased indebtedness, which do not belong here, will not be included in parliament's final reading. we have therefore voted in favour of the proposal for rules on monitoring and assessing draft budgetary plans and ensuring the correction of excessive deficits in the euro area member states.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'common carrier' in the definition of the term 'commercial vehicle'. the proposal contains many important tools to ensure budgetary discipline among euro area members, which has been underlined by developments in recent years. in the process, proposals for joint borrowing and an eu investment fund equivalent to 1% of gdp have been put forward, which would only lead to further debt accumulation. we have voted against these individual parts. the commission proposal, on the other hand, concerns instruments and frameworks which it is urgent to decide on, especially in the present situation. we have therefore voted in favour of the remaining parts of the proposal. we assume that the parts dealing with increased indebtedness, which do not belong here, will not be included in parliament's final reading. we have therefore voted in favour of the proposal for rules on monitoring and assessing draft budgetary plans and ensuring the correction of excessive deficits in the euro area member states.
in writing. - europe is facing an important crossroads. it is about deepening cooperation towards deeper political and economic integration or asking some countries to leave the euro. we are convinced that the way out of the crisis is through deeper political and economic cooperation. to achieve this, economic policies are needed that lead to sound and stable public finances and higher economic growth. we therefore welcome the two legislative proposals, the two-pack, which further tighten the economic surveillance of the euro area. the rules on budgetary surveillance need to be tightened up and followed by countries with large budget deficits and excessive public debt. the liberal group is in favour of the introduction of a debt-reduction fund. we support the idea because such a fund can create reasonable conditions for the countries with high public debt to remain in the euro area. the fund must have strict conditions and run for a limited period in order to function. it cannot therefore be compared with the introduction of eurobonds. the debt reduction fund can be an important piece of the puzzle in solving the debt crisis in europe and should be carefully evaluated.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'common carrier' to cover all transport services. the resolution also calls for the eu to be able to take action to combat terrorism and to ensure that the eu is able to take action to combat terrorism. own resources are a tax on citizens. if the eu is given the right to set taxes, the balance of power between the member states and the eu will change. we do not want to transfer sweden's taxation rights to a majority in the council and the european parliament. the eu can meet its commitments, new responsibilities and at the same time create the conditions for jobs and growth by prioritising. more money for the eu in the form of own resources, transaction taxes and new vat is not an investment that will help europe out of the crisis or the right way to finance the next long-term eu budget.
in writing. − i cannot support the resolution on the ‘multiannual financial framework and own resources’ as it gives credence to the austerity agenda in the member states. furthermore, i cannot support its call for an increase in the eu’s own resources, which is aimed at reducing the share of member-state-based contributions, as this will diminish the democratic accountability of the eu as well as the redistributive nature of the financing system. my decision was consequently to vote against the resolution. however, i support the parliament’s role in the process and my vote is not in any way a support for the council’s proposal.
in writing. - we believe that the eu budget must have sufficient resources to finance the commitments it has made. at the same time, we would stress that the eu should, as far as possible, take a restrictive stance and provide these resources through reallocation within the existing eu budget. we would prefer to see the agricultural budget reduced in favour of the priorities set out in the europe 2020 strategy: research and development, green transition, regional development, etc. we also believe it is very important to make eu administrative expenditure more efficient, one way of doing this being to increase transparency in the process of allocating and using this expenditure. we are open to a reform of the current rebate system which is enormously complicated if such a reform contributes to more transparency and provided that sweden is not discriminated against. we do not, however, believe that this should be conditional on the adoption of a new longterm budget. we also do not think that new own resources should be introduced. however, a change in this system does not imply any direct taxation right for the eu; the union does not have such a competence in the treaty. any change to the own resources system requires the approval of all member states in order to enter into force.
the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the euro as a currency. it is one of the pillars of the budgetary process and is also the one that will guide the distribution of the common funds at the disposal of the union. so far, everything is fine. unfortunately, the resolution also contains some texts which cannot be supported in the same way. they are about welcoming the financial transaction tax, which i firmly believe is a waste for sweden and europe, and about new own resources for the eu. own resources too often mean a tax to be paid directly to the eu budget. more money for the eu in the form of own resources is not automatically an investment that will help europe out of its troubles and strains. nor is it reasonable to ignore the difficult economic considerations and problems of individual quite a number of nations in the context of the eu budget. despite the very problematic wording, as i see it, i chose to vote in favour of the resolution.
member of the commission. - mr president, i thank mr klaus-heiner lehne for what he said and for the initiative he took in this respect. i promise him that on this issue, as on all others, i am wide awake, mr president, and proactive. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the internal market. it is the immediate, everyday environment of all businesses in europe. our aim is to create, through this law and other reforms, the most efficient 'ecosystem' for business work, european mobility. i would like to say again that this large market should not be made first or only for large companies, but also for small and mediumsized enterprises, or even for very small enterprises. these are very real issues. that is why i was pleased with the success of the consultation on company law. we received over 500 responses and president lehne also referred to the contribution of the european parliament from all economic, social and academic actors across europe. we are objectively analysing the answers we have received. at this stage, what i understand from the responses we are receiving, and then from my own convictions, is that we have very strong support for european initiatives in this area of company law, particularly on two points. firstly, to facilitate crossborder business operations. i am thinking of the improvement of the regime for transnational mergers, the measures on crossborder divisions, or the rather difficult question of crossborder transfer of headquarters. secondly, the codification of company law. we're working on it. it is a long process, on which i hope, before the end of my mandate, to be able to make you concrete proposals. on the basis of these results, mr lehne, i am preparing, ladies and gentlemen, for october next year, an action plan with a series of concrete short, medium and longterm initiatives in the field of company law. and you will find, mr lehne, in this plan almost all the points you yourself have mentioned. but i'll go back to a few of them. this plan will cover our thinking on the future of company law and also our current work on corporate governance. i am convinced that we cannot separate the two, governance and corporate law. the two work streams are therefore grouped together to create this coherent framework. i would also like to welcome the work already done on corporate governance by your parliament, in particular by your rapporteur, mr bodu, whom i thank. i do not intend, of course, to go into detail about what this plan will cover, but i would like to mention some of the areas of work by telegraph. first, the plan will include initiatives to create more transparency between companies and investors. we must learn from certain irresponsible behaviours or practices that we have condemned. a first initiative will focus on non-financial corporate transparency, with the key element of requiring companies to communicate their diversity policy on boards. i will also propose actions to encourage more constructive and longer-term shareholder involvement in companies. in another area, i intend to propose that you hold shareholders accountable, for example in setting the framework for remuneration in the financial sector. another aspect of this plan is that i would like to propose measures to boost the competitiveness of european enterprises. there is a very strong demand to facilitate cross-border transactions. in this context, i understood the meaning of the parliament's resolution on a 14th directive on the crossborder transfer of the registered office of companies. there are quite different opinions on this question. the question of a new proposal remains open, but it is clear that i will only want to embark on this path on the basis of clear support from the member states and the european parliament. in the light of the results of our consultation, i will ask my services to reassess the situation and, if necessary, to prepare a proposal for a directive. i would also like to say a word, mr lehne, on a point you have raised, namely the european private sector. it is a subject which is stalled in the council, where unanimity has not been achieved so far, despite the efforts of several presidencies and my own efforts. it is an important tool to help european small and mediumsized enterprises and i do not want to exclude any way of advancing it, including, mr lehne, the means of enhanced cooperation, which can be used if a sufficient number of member states wish it, and that is what i am going to check. i would like to say that i am ready to support closer cooperation on this issue. ladies and gentlemen, i am increasingly convinced that europe needs a regulatory framework adapted to the changing economic environment, to the needs of today's society. this regulatory framework should make it easier for companies to develop in the union, for the benefit of their shareholders and customers and also their employees. i am aware that some recent initiatives in the area of company law have run into difficulties. that's no reason for me to shut the door and do nothing. we shall therefore prepare this plan, in the spirit i have just indicated, in a fairly complete and fairly precise manner. that is why i am also looking forward with great interest to your discussions on your resolution on the future of company law. it will arrive at the right time to inform our work and give more solidity, and no doubt also ambition, to the action plan which i shall present to you in a few months' time.
for the alde group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. recently, the debate on europe has focused on how best to get out of the economic crisis - necessarily. savings and financial responsibility have been pitted against measures to stimulate growth. in political rhetoric, i think, unfortunately, that very often we miss the fundamental question, which is: how can we ensure the development and prosperity of the eu beyond the crisis? as we know, prosperity does not come from nowhere. it can only happen when people in creativity create goods and services. one of the most important things we can do in the policy arena to create jobs and growth is to break down barriers to trade and to break down barriers to entrepreneurship and trade in general and to ensure that entrepreneurs and innovators can devote their creativity to developing their businesses. this is precisely what today's resolution is about. there are positive reforms in european company law and i would like to thank commissioner michel barnier for this good proposal. these are important steps to strengthen competitiveness, especially of smes in europe. we must dare to keep alive the vision of a europe beyond the crisis: fewer barriers to trade, but more simple crossborder corporate law regulations. i would like to join my colleague, klaus-heiner lehne, in urging the commission to look at this carefully and to come back with further strong proposals along these lines.
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. we need to reform european company law. the fragmentation of european company and tax law today creates the situation that it is primarily member states that appear to compete in the european market, rather than companies competing with each other for customers. we also need to correct the situation where companies use this fragmented legislation for extensive tax planning. we know from the discussion on the audit and transparency directives that many member states and companies have strongly resisted project-based auditing and instead focused on reporting their investments on a national basis. it creates very high risks of assets being moved from the european union to tax havens in third countries and this is a situation that cannot be remedied mainly because of the resistance of the member states. i am particularly concerned that the council of ministers' reactions to parliament's proposals are generally poor. we have seen in connection with the transaction tax and the consolidated tax base that as soon as parliament takes a decision in the right direction, the council of ministers tries to prevent it. i am naturally wondering how we can remedy this from the parliament's side and what the commission can do to make the council of ministers stop its contradictions.
the s&d group is in favour of the proposal. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr jános áder, commissioner john dalli, the danish presidency and all the other rapporteurs from the various political groups for the good cooperation. it has been important for my group to maintain and, preferably, strengthen the protection of people and the environment when we are now revising this directive. i think we have succeeded in that ambition. all seveso installations will be required under seveso iii to develop a major accident prevention policy (mapp) to be implemented through a safety management system. this is the case unless member states decide otherwise on the basis of a risk analysis. this is a step forward. the agreement strengthens the requirements for internal and external rescue plans, the requirements for member states to carry out inspections, the requirements to take account of 'domino effects' - i.e. external factors which may aggravate the accident - and the information to the public is somewhat improved. certain information on the activities shall always be available electronically, including for the activities belonging to the lower security classification. but my group would have liked to go a little further in terms of protecting the environment and people by extending the scope of the directive. we would also like to improve the information available to the public. it is also not optimal that ordinary legislation will now be required to change the criteria governing which activities are covered by seveso iii. but since the council could not accept to change this by means of delegated acts, this had to be the solution. on the whole, we think this is a good agreement and we will vote for it tomorrow.
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to thank jános áder and wish him every success in his new role as president of hungary. i think it is a pity that the council is not here, because i think they should have tried to defend publicly what they stood for in the negotiations, namely a cynical zero-sum game where re-classification of chemicals does not lead to increased protection, but you have a zero-sum game on the number of plants you want to have covered by the directive instead of looking at the health benefits and the protection against accidents. it was a shame that they had that attitude. in the end, however, we got an agreement that actually works better in many ways, as åsa westlund and others have commented. it was particularly important that we did not exclude whole categories of chemicals which the council initially wanted. however, a number of tasks remain for the commission, including the investigation of the insurance requirement. i think it is very important that the commission now address this. it is not possible that the costs should be overwhelmed in general when these companies are not able to keep their facilities intact, clean and tidy, but they have to insure against it. it shouldn't be the taxpayers' burden.

in writing. − the resolution on breast implants proposes that breast implant surgeries be registered in the eu in the form of a mandatory national register in each member state. the register will make reporting mandatory for all clinics while patients will have the right to consent to the entry of their personal data. the purpose of the registers is to allow the exchange of information, for example, in the event of serious defects in the implants. we swedish social democrats want to emphasize the voluntary nature of the register. it is thus up to each individual patient to decide whether he or she wants to be included in the register. that premise enabled us to vote for the resolution as a whole.
the s&d group is in favour of the proposal. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i welcome the revision of the regulation and i would like to thank jürgen creutzmann for his good cooperation with us as shadow rapporteur. many of the goods covered by this regulation are intended for direct consumption, such as food, medicinal products and toys. in some cases, they can be directly dangerous to health and life. the s&d group has therefore taken as its starting point the safeguarding of the internal market for the benefit of our consumers. for example, we have excluded goods imported for private use and parallel imports from the scope of the regulation. in the imco committee we supported the idea of including goods in transit in the regulation. unfortunately, in the imco, juri and inta committees have decided to table a number of amendments to ensure that access to generic medicines in developing countries is not impeded by this decision. the main amendment is to exclude goods in transit from the scope of the directive, except where there is clear and convincing evidence that they are destined for the eu market. as a consequence of our position on generics, we will also vote against jürgen creutzman's proposal for a simplified procedure for all types of infringing goods, as this simplified procedure should not apply to goods other than on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, we all agree that counterfeit goods are a bad thing and something that we do not want in the european union, but i would remind all colleagues that we already have lots of legislation on that and that we already have the customs regulation. the new thing in this report is that it extends the responsibility of customs from – as the situation is now – stopping counterfeit goods (that is, goods with a falsified trade mark) to handling all sorts of intellectual property including, for instance, patents. that is not something that the customs office is equipped to do. patent conflicts are incredibly complex; we even have a special court structure to sort out whether a patent is infringing or not. it is not something a customs official can do just like that. this of course leads to problems, in particular for generic medicines. the organisations that are working on access to medicines are very concerned with this report, and they say that, if it goes through in the way it is drafted now, it will be bad for access to medicines. but i would also raise another issue: the focus on small consignments. i think that has a great chance of really harming confidence in e-trade, which is something we want more of. an ordinary customer who orders a smartphone and has it confiscated and destroyed by the customs office because there is a patent conflict between samsung and apple will never understand why that happened and will completely lose confidence in e-trade. i think this report needs to be seriously redrafted if we are to accept it.
madam president, i would like to thank you. thank you very much for a good report on an important subject. i would like to raise the issue of chemicals and other foreign substances in the water. in my home town of stockholm, people were warned not to wet the floors because the waste water they got in their bucket contains so many dangerous chemicals that when you pour the water into the toilet seat, or wherever you pour it, the sewage treatment plant simply cannot handle the water properly. it's too toxic. it means that all around us, in the dust and everywhere, there are many dangerous chemicals, and those chemicals we need to take responsibility for, to get rid of these products so that we can have clean water in the future. of course, better cleaning can be demanded and certainly needs to be done, but the most important thing - and this report also shows this - is to tackle pollution at source, i.e. to remove dangerous chemicals and, for example, silver in clothes, which are washed out and end up in our water instead.
the rapporteur. - madam president! - what is it? commissioners and colleagues! it has been an intense but fantastic job to bring about this animal welfare strategy which i must say is well balanced. the shadow rapporteurs have done a very good job, our staff have done a fantastic job and we have managed to get along with everyone from animal welfare organisations to farmers and so on. there are still some sensitive points in this discussion, and one of them is the legal milestones, where we have concluded that the commission needs the possibility of monitoring the member states during a transitional period. remember what happened to the chicken cages? it was a ten-year transitional period, and the commission could not do anything until 2 january this year when the ten years had already passed. there must be some points where we can check and question what the member states are doing to implement legislation with transitional periods. it's these milestones that are a little controversial. the other thing that's very controversial -- and we've had many, many questions about this -- is the deaf-mute slaughter. many people want us to label the meat as unprocessed or untreated. i have to say, personally, i think it's the wrong way to go. first of all, let us make sure that the religious exception, which is very small and very precise, is not abused. i think that anyone who abuses this exception will never write on the slaughtered body that this is an animal that was slaughtered without anesthesia. the third piece of controversy is the domesticated wildlife, the stray dogs and stray cats. it's a problem in many countries, and it's very problematic how to deal with it. in our document we place the responsibility primarily on the member states and on local and regional authorities. we also ask that all domestic animals, i.e. dogs and cats, etc., be marked and registered. but the most important thing in this proposal is a general animal welfare law which is similar in structure to the general food law. this law covers all animals in captivity, that is, all animals except those in the wild. it shall contain clear definitions of what good animal welfare means and who is responsible for it. there has to be a dissemination of knowledge, and there has to be - to counter the counter-proposal that is about cloning - a specification in the law that no breeding should take place so that the animal cannot live a natural life, and this applies to everything from cows to dogs. finally, a general law and general rules provide free and fair competition for all farmers throughout the union, and the last point is very important.
mr president, europe stands before one of the most difficult challenges ever: to get out of the worst economic crisis of the post-war period and at the same time take the european project forward. this is not an easy task; if it is to succeed, the eu and its member states must start conducting a more balanced economic policy – single-minded austerity is not enough. the eu must also seriously attack the problem that for decades we have set aside insufficient resources for growth-enhancing investments. therefore i am very happy with the broad support for my report on project bonds, to be issued tomorrow. i am also happy about the european council conclusions on the compact for growth and jobs and the detailed guidelines for the union’s own actions and budget in this context. eur 55 billion will be devoted to growth-enhancing measures in the current period. however, there is still a question mark: to have concrete effects on the ground, the eu budget will need payments. these eur 120 billion, with very concrete proposals for eur 55 billion, must lead to concrete projects with rapid effects on jobs and growth, particularly in the most vulnerable regions in europe. but for that to happen, the council and the member states must change their rigid position on the payments level in the european union budget, because otherwise – unfortunately – this will be another round of empty promises.
in writing. the european parliament has also called for the establishment of a european union-wide network of information centres for the protection of the environment. we are in favour of macro-regional strategies as a tool for promoting the eu's territorial cohesion and development. however, we do not support the calls to raise the thresholds for the minimum rates for islands. we also do not believe that the scope of the minimum periods should be extended to the transport sector, or to the agricultural and fisheries sectors.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the transport of dangerous goods by rail. however, we do not believe that a common railway area based solely on deregulation and competition will solve the problems we face. bad examples of too harsh deregulation of the railways in, for example, sweden and other countries show this. we do not believe that further opening up of, for example, domestic passenger traffic is in the interests of europe. what sweden and other european countries need is increased investment in infrastructure and a common traveller perspective. addressing cross-border capacity problems requires new tracks, reduced sensitivity to interference and more modern electrification through new technologies.
in writing. - we moderates voted today against the report on road transport recorders. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the automatic recording of location data by a global navigation satellite system (gnss). from a privacy perspective, we question the usefulness of being able to locate drivers around europe. the proposal also provides for the use of similar technologies by companies in europe. many companies have now developed their own good concepts for tachographs, and we are concerned that the eu is locking itself into some particular technology and that the development of the companies' products has been unnecessarily costly. we are also against the proposal to introduce driving licences with a driver card chip to be connected to a tachograph. this would be an unnecessarily expensive and bureaucratic change.
in writing. - i welcome the fact that parliament voted to simplify procedures for customs authorities and to strengthen customs controls on the enforcement of intellectual property rights. it is important to promote trade, while at the same time we should ensure that dangerous goods do not enter the eu market. in addition, pirated and counterfeit goods impose huge costs on eu businesses every year. i therefore welcome the clarification of issues such as the cost of storage and destruction, the handling of goods in transit and simplified procedures for small consignments. it is important to emphasize in particular that we have succeeded in clearly pushing through the fact that the regulation should not apply to goods of a non-commercial nature contained in travellers' personal luggage.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'e-infrastructure'. the purpose of ecall is to make it easier for emergency services to arrive quickly at the scene of an accident by means of computerisation. it's supposed to be able to track cars that get into traffic accidents. in the event of an accident, the nearest emergency centre in europe will be notified. but the service is already on the market, several car brands are already producing cars with this service. we therefore believe that the eu does not need to decide on this. the installation of ecall would lead to increased costs for drivers, as cars would become more expensive to manufacture. it should be up to the individual motorist to decide whether he wants this service in his car and thus also pay for a more expensive car. from a privacy point of view, this also means that drivers can be monitored. if you as a consumer choose this system, it is a voluntary contract between the customer and the company. but if the eu decides to do so, you are forced to be monitored, something we moderates felt we could not support.
member of the commission. − madam president, honourable members, tomorrow, you will have to make a choice. it is an important choice for at least two reasons. firstly, because the debate over the anti-counterfeiting trade agreement has involved much discussion of its relationship with the fundamental rights and freedoms of european citizens. secondly, because of the signal this vote will send to our trading partners and to the more than 120 million workers that work in europe’s innovative, manufacturing and creative sectors. they are dependent for their livelihoods on the effective enforcement of intellectual property rights, in europe and across the rest of the world. the commission has asked the european court of justice for its opinion on whether the anti-counterfeiting trade agreement is compatible with the treaties and, in particular, with our charter of fundamental rights. we have done this because these questions are crucial for those who took to the streets over acta earlier this year. we share their view that these concerns must be addressed. that is why the acta agreement should be the subject of the most careful scrutiny and deliberation. in this respect, i believe that it would have been preferable for parliament to wait until we know what the court thinks before voting. but that is not where we are today. tomorrow you will only be able to choose to vote ‘yes’ or ‘no’ to the agreement. let me then just say that my considered view, as a lifelong supporter of human rights and fundamental freedoms, is that there is nothing to fear in this agreement. acta is not an attack on our liberties, it is a defence of our livelihoods. this is because we do not have to modify any part of our internal legislation, the so-called acquis communautaire. what is legal today in the european union will remain legal once acta is ratified. and what is illegal today will still remain illegal with acta. nothing changes in the eyes of the law. and since our freedoms are not threatened by our current laws, our freedoms will not be threatened by acta. this is also – i might add – the view of the european parliament’s own legal service. i hope that you will bear these considerations in mind. economic consequences are the second reason the vote is important. we all agree that counterfeiting and piracy are a serious problem. they risk undermining one of europe’s most significant competitive advantages over other regions of the world, namely our knowledge-based and creative industries. acta is designed to extend the tools for enforcement of intellectual property rights that have been so successful in europe – and which this parliament has always supported – to other countries as well. no more. no less. so, as you come to make your choice about how to vote tomorrow, you should also make no mistake: a vote against acta will be a setback for the protection of our intellectual property rights around the world. neither are there any quick fixes for its rejection. those who think that we can come back any time soon with a revised agreement or with a new treaty have been misled. what is true is that, if parliament votes this treaty down, the commission will continue to wait for the opinion of the court and study it closely. why? because citizens have raised concerns over its potential impact on fundamental rights, because many of you have raised similar questions – so let us get some answers. i consider it my obligation, my responsibility as european commissioner – indeed the responsibility of us all – to ask for clarity from europe’s highest court. furthermore, we will also look at how the debate on intellectual property rights evolves over the coming months. there are legitimate issues to be discussed and clarified about some rules on intellectual property rights in the digital environment. for instance, the definition of ‘commercial scale’ and what sharing information means in relation to the challenges one faces with respect to the protection of intellectual property. i hope that these discussions will be able to happen in their proper context – which is in debates about the substantive law of the european union, not about international enforcement. besides the european parliament, the commission would also discuss the outcome of the court referral with other signatories of acta and would then consider further steps to take. as europeans, i believe that we all share profound respect for individual freedom. but i also know that freedom needs a framework. what we are doing both with acta and with european legislation is making sure that the framework strikes the right balance. it is a delicate task and we must proceed carefully.
the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy has tabled a number of amendments to the proposal. - madam president! - what is it? thank you to all my colleagues and to all those listening out in europe. for those of us who have been involved in the debate on information policy for some time, the decision which this house is to take tomorrow is a symbol not only of what is right and reasonable in the information age, but it is also a crucial issue for european democracy. communication and culture build societies, so it is no wonder that acta became the issue that created the pan-european debate that has captured our attention and made many people think about how we as a society really want to deal with communication. it was in this question that i and many others in my political party, the pirate party, found the basis of our political commitment. the problem is, nothing changes with the deal. we need more access to culture and to communication. how do we really want to relate to other people? how do we build community and intimacy with other people? all of us who are thinking about these issues and who are involved in building these communities hope that tomorrow's decision will be taken by a strengthened parliament - a parliament that listens to all european citizens when they speak with one voice. for our societies must be built on the values that our citizens have. it is clear that the values of citizens mean that communication and cultural exchange are a priority and an opportunity for the future, and that is what my group and i in this parliament, together with many other colleagues from other political groups, will be confirming at the votes tomorrow.
for the ppe group. president. - the debate is closed. tomorrow we are voting on the anti-counterfeiting trade agreement, and let me first of all be clear about what i and the ppe group think about this. we need international cooperation to defend intellectual property rights, to stop counterfeit medicines and car parts, to stop infringement of european patents and piracy of european products, and to safeguard european competitiveness. the acta is a good basis for that work. but it would be irresponsible to vote on acta tomorrow to take a position before we have all the cards on the table and before the european court of justice has ruled on the agreement. that's not a serious and responsible way to handle this issue. to all those who object to waiting for the court, i would like to ask: why would you do that? what is it about the european parliament that you are so afraid of the court of justice saying that you have to vote before the highest court of the european union has spoken? tomorrow's vote is not just about acta. it is about patient safety, research, aviation safety, competitiveness, the environment, safety at work, etc. for these areas are plagued by piracy, patent infringement and counterfeiting. voting no tomorrow therefore carries a great responsibility. it may solve the conflict over acta, but it is not an answer to the problems and challenges facing europe. voting no is not the status quo either. it weakens europe's voice in the world. how can we convince india to take strong action against counterfeit medicines if we have refused european or international cooperation based on our own legislation? that those in this room who don't want intellectual property rights or don't think we should protect intellectual property rights are voting against acta before we have a court ruling, that doesn't surprise me. it is not surprising that those who do not want europe to fight climate change do not want the kyoto protocol either. but i do not think that the majority of us members of parliament hold that view, and that is why i hope that you will not want to die on acta before we have all the cards on the table. why would you do that? we need international cooperation on intellectual property rights to defend european jobs, consumers, patients and so on. that is why we will not reject acta tomorrow. mr president, welcome to the european parliament. we look forward to the cyprus presidency. as leader of the socialist group in the committee on budgets, i am, like anne e. jensen, primarily interested in the presidency's plans for the negotiations on the next long-term budget of the european union, the financial framework. it is becoming increasingly clear that some strong net-paying countries have formed a pact to reduce the eu budget for the future. their ideas of economic policy seem to be based on austerity as the only way out of the crisis. they believe that richer member states should not support poorer countries with problems through the eu budget, as they are considered to be mismanaging. on the other hand, there are also a number of member states whose perspective is closer to mine. they realise that the eu budget cannot be compared with national budgets dominated by public consumption such as social transfers, public employees' salaries, etc. the eu budget is a budget for solidarity and investment, a necessary strategic part of a common european path towards [...] a strategically crucial role in linking europe, completing the internal market in areas such as research, innovation and infrastructure. so my question to you, mr. president, is this: what is your strategy for getting these member states with such different perspectives to agree on an offensive vision of the eu for the future and thus lay the foundations for an agreement with the european parliament?
rapporteur. − mr president, this bilateral agreement is very necessary although not perfect. by joining the wto, russia has committed to gradually reducing or eliminating its existing export duties on a list of raw materials. however, not all the essential goods are covered. for that reason, the eu has negotiated this agreement. a key achievement is russia’s commitment not to impose new or additional export duties. in the event it would like to do so, russia should consult the european commission at least two months in advance. there are also a few shortcomings. firstly, the wording in the agreement does not make it binding enough. secondly, no bilateral dispute settlement mechanism is foreseen. there are not too many tools for the eu to enforce this agreement if russia does not fulfil its commitments. therefore, this agreement should be seen as a temporary solution until it is replaced by a legally binding agreement included in the new partnership and cooperation agreement. let me conclude by saying that the agreement is an important step towards stabilising the trade relationship between the eu and russia. it does not eliminate unpredictable behaviour from russia, but nevertheless will improve trade relations. i call on you, dear colleagues, to support the agreement by voting in favour of it.
mr president, under rule 175(2) of the rules of procedure i would like, on behalf of the epp group, to refer this back to the committee on international trade. the reason is quite obvious and should be considered by anybody who wants to treat this agreement seriously. the anti-counterfeiting trade agreement which gives us international cooperation to fight piracy, counterfeit and other ipr infringements is currently being evaluated by the european court of justice. as much of the criticism against this agreement relates to fundamental rights – exactly what the court has been asked by the commission to look into and assess – we would not like to take the final vote in plenary before we have had their view on this important topic. therefore the epp group would like to refer this back to the committee on international trade.
in writing. − i abstained on the agriculture reports on alignment. these reports align the existing body of agricultural legislation to the new legal reality, which is the system of delegated and implementing acts. reports which use delegated acts are those on which the parliament has a right of veto. although i am opposed to the common agricultural policy, i recognise that the principle of protecting parliament’s right of veto, where it has been legally given (in the lisbon treaty), is a principle that needs to be protected. this principle will reach beyond agricultural reports. because of this, because i do not wish the commission to make decisions in which parliament should co-legislate, i abstained on this report.
in writing. - we have chosen to support this report because we consider the proposed increase in eu payments next year to be reasonable in relation to the eu's existing commitments. to turn a blind eye to these, and to the challenges facing the community, would be irresponsible. the trend of continuously transferring payments to the budget of the following year must be broken. at the same time, however, we have supported the amendment calling for the work of the european parliament to be moved to a single location, and the amendment calling for a thorough examination of the european added value of every investment made. this is especially important in these times when several countries are in economic crisis. we believe, however, that these times call for even more action on the part of the eu, and that the eu must therefore be given sufficient resources to be able to fulfil its commitments. on the contrary, the choice at eu level to 'slow down' would not be the right one.
in writing. - i voted in favour of this report because i believe that animal welfare is important. it goes without saying that we should treat our animals with respect. we will take account of producers and small businesses and not create extra bureaucracy and administrative costs. swedish producers must be able to compete on equal terms and we need a common regulatory framework and raise standards throughout europe. it is important that we have clear common rules which do not mislead producers. the report is important because we need to reduce the use of antibiotics in our livestock farming. in the united states, for example, more antibiotics are used in animals than in humans. if we can have a sustainable environment for our animals, we can also reduce the spread of infection and, in the long run, reduce our dependence on antibiotics. i hope that we will begin to see the seriousness of these problems in the future and i am therefore pleased that the report addresses the link between animal welfare and the spread of infectious and communicable diseases. at the same time, i am glad that the report emphasises the need to implement existing legislation rather than new rules. there are still many countries which have not succeeded in introducing the existing rules, and this creates different conditions for competition in our internal market.
in writing. - we voted in favour of the alternative resolution on animal welfare and animal protection, in which we support the citizens' initiative on a maximum of eight hours of animal transport; however, we oppose the text in the alternative resolution, as well as in the report by the committee on agriculture, which calls for the same animal welfare standards for animals and products imported from third countries as for european products, and for future free trade agreements to be made subject to these conditions.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of animals in the context of the implementation of the directive.
in writing. the european parliament has already adopted a resolution on the situation in the middle east. this is because the text of the resolution contains both positive and negative elements. on the one hand, we welcome the reorientation of european crisis management that took place at the summit. the counterproductive austerity policy was then definitively replaced by a more balanced policy line combining budgetary discipline with an offensive commitment to growth and jobs - something we have made strong demands on. in particular, the new growth pact with the capital increase of the eib, the reallocation of the remaining structural funds resources and the pilot phase of the project bonds is an important step in the right direction. the new policy direction now creates opportunities to devise a long-term sustainable solution to the crisis. on the other hand, we are opposed to the writings on the emu report by mr van rompuy, which was discussed at the summit. some of the reforms identified in the report could be valuable in the euro area countries' efforts to establish the single currency. other reforms are doubtful. we also do not believe that the adoption of a new long-term budget should be conditional on a reform of the own resources system.
in writing. the european parliament has already adopted a resolution on the situation in the former yugoslavia, which calls for the immediate release of all political prisoners. the resolution stresses the importance of a strong framework for the public finances of the member states and that action should be taken to complete the internal market. these are the two most important components for europe to emerge from the current crisis, and that is why we voted in favour of the resolution. at the same time, we continue to oppose the own resources levies and the various forms of joint debt issuance which also appear in the resolution.
in writing. - i've lowered my voice. the report calls for guaranteeing access to basic payment services for all consumers legally resident in the union. that is a demand i support. but i am opposed to the eu deciding this at supranational level by means of a directive. in this situation, i choose to keep my voice down.
the rapporteur. - mr president! - what is it? my colleagues! this title on investment in transeuropean networks, etc., actually obscures the very interesting report on project bonds. maybe it should have come out of the headline, but it doesn't. the economic future of europe is not just about tackling the acute crisis. it is just as much about accelerating the sustainable development of the economy and jobs in the long term and, above all, increasing growth-generating investment, both private and public. the eu's role is crucial here. to bring together the different parts of europe and to develop the potential that is actually being created by 500 million people in a single internal market, we need to be able to make strategic investments: in transport, in modern infrastructure, in the energy sector, in the digital sector, etc. it is absolutely crucial to our success in achieving our goals of smart and sustainable growth for all. the economic crisis is not only a consequence of the banking and financial collapse, but also of the fact that we have had an insufficient level of european investment for a long time. that is why the investment needs are now enormous. in the transport, energy and information technology sectors, the commission estimates the need for eur 1 500 billion by 2020. this is a huge amount of money that the eu cannot mobilise on its own. the majority of the funding is therefore, of course, provided by the private sector, but also by national, regional and local authorities. however, with today's severe restrictions on public budgets, the tightening of capital requirements for banks, etc., we need to find new ways forward. one of these paths is the development of innovative financial instruments, which, with some eu budget support, should be able to attract additional capital. project bonds are one such instrument. the proposal is to enable project companies to issue bonds, which will be more secure and attractive on the market, through eib loans guaranteed by the eu budget. the eu's action should be able to raise the credit rating of these projects so that it becomes interesting for pension funds, for example, to participate. however, it is important to emphasise that these are private bonds issued by project companies, and not eu public bonds. the commission's proposal, which the committee on budgets supports, means that we are reallocating eur 230 million from the eu budget for the 2012-13 project bonds. it is not a lot of money, but the commission expects that this money will be able to mobilise perhaps 15 to 20 times that amount from other actors, and then it will be substantial. that part of the effort is maximised to the amount of the guarantee provided for in the budget, and there will therefore be no increase in eu expenditure. if all goes well, the cost will be zero, but we will help to accelerate growth in europe. but we must remember that project bonds are only one piece of this investment puzzle, where they must be evaluated. i hope that they will be able to develop further in the way that president barroso of the commission, but also, for example, the eu summit and the g20 have pointed out in recent weeks. i would also like to highlight other ideas. i think we should look at whether, in the analysis and evaluation, by improving this proposal, we can reach new types of investors. can we reach smes through, for example, project portfolios? can we help create a more liquid bond market in europe? perhaps we could even think a little more about the question of so-called delors bonds? european project bonds guaranteed by the eu budget? i think we should at least discuss it. finally, a big thank you to all those who have helped to forge this important solution: my colleagues in the committee on budgets, in particular the shadow rapporteurs, the european commission, the european investment bank and last but not least the danish presidency. we now hope that the cypriot presidency will finally be able to bring the matter to a conclusion.
rapporteur. − mr president, colleagues, thank you very much for this enlightening debate. let me just conclude with a few comments. first, on the need for the instrument. insufficient levels of investment are a major problem for europe. this is a structural rather than temporary problem, and the present crisis has made it much more difficult to deal with and to change the situation. that is why we need new additional instruments. what kind of projects will be financed? i agree with mr hoyer; to begin with the focus will probably be on more developed markets. i warmly thank president hoyer for pointing out that a wider perspective may be possible in the future, when the instrument will be able to target other things as well. that is also very important. on criteria, mr turmes and some others have raised the idea of having other kinds of projects, such as renewable energy projects. i am quite open to that. let us have a discussion for the coming multiannual financial framework on a more permanent phase for the financial instruments and project bonds. i am quite open to this idea with regard to the evaluation of this pilot phase. i would say that the main discussion here has been about the risks. i know that some member states, including my own, have been reluctant to accept this initiative but i hope that this debate has reduced their worries. we are not taking any unreasonable risks with taxpayers’ money and we really need this additional effort. do we socialise risk and privatise profits? i would say that with these guarantees we will be able to mobilise resources which otherwise would stay in the coffers or end up in the speculative markets. it is taxpayers who gain the most from such an approach. the risk is strictly limited. there are no contingent liabilities. if we are given a choice between using eur 230 million for grants, which will lead to investments of only eur 230 million, and using eur 230 million as an innovative financial instrument which might mobilise billions, then we will see that the way we are taking now is quite good. i began by thanking colleagues; let me also say a big ‘thank you’ to the committee on budgets. the secretariat there has done a fantastic job, and so has my own office.
mr president, i would like to thank you. commissioner, i share your view that rio did not go as far as we had hoped. to me, it's an empty shell. i remember rio 20 years ago when it inspired all of us to take on the problems at the local level. i think that's where we're going again. we can't wait for global leaders to stop listening to corporations and suddenly start acting fast enough. we can only solve the challenges we face if we start from the bottom and work our way up. that people in their everyday lives are beginning to deal with our planet and our economy in a different way. we have a completely unsustainable economy that is built entirely on non-renewable resources, and a massive shift to a green economy does not mean green growth. green growth is an illusion based on completely unrealistic images of resource efficiency. it is absolutely impossible to achieve, and that is why we need another economy that is actually green. the only way to get that is to measure other targets. if you measure only economic activity, that's what you get. if we measure other goals, we get this instead: a better planet. let's go that way.
mr president, i would like to thank you. commissioner, what is it? the first un conference on environment and sustainable development was held in my home city of stockholm. that was 40 years ago, and a lot has happened since then. today, for example, there is a very broad knowledge and awareness of the environment and sustainable development. roger helmer of the efd group is quite alone in his views, most others are aware that there are very big things happening in the environmental field that we have to deal with. the problem is that the measures we have decided on so far at international or national level have not been able to hold back the rapid development but the continuing increase in environmental and climate pressures. many are disappointed in the rio summit, and i ask the question: what were we thinking? do we think it is possible to create political pressure on long-term survival issues when the economic crisis forces so many people to worry about how to make ends meet in the short term? did we really think we could do this? for that reason, i think the rio meeting has been a wake-up call, because it has clearly linked social justice issues with climate and environmental issues, and we must take that into account in our future work. if we are to move forward, we must always link social justice now with our common survival concerns in the long run.
mr president, i would like to thank you. i am, like many colleagues, depressed that the rio text does not contain more concrete commitments. we know we're already living beyond our means. if everyone in the world lived like a swede, for example, we would consume 3.25 of the globe. obviously, this insight wasn't enough, but we need to be more resource efficient. historically, growth has come largely because we've had cheap raw materials and cheap energy, but that's not going to be able to continue. so it's important for us to show that we can break the link between higher living standards and increased environmental degradation. this can be done today. for example, we know that it is profitable to make energy efficient, and i think it is time for us to show that this is possible. it is possible to live a good life and at the same time live sustainably. that is how we can attract others to the international stage; not by threats or intimidation, but by really showing that it is possible and does not have to involve major social problems. on the contrary, it is the solution to many social problems.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, the eu regulation on illegal, unregulated and unreported (iuu) fishing was a major step forward globally in the battle against iuu. the commission has been working very hard to convince both its member states and third countries about how to implement the requirements of catch documents signed by the flag states, and some countries have been lobbying extremely hard to have exemptions from this rule, in order to let chartering states have the right to issue catch documents. the concept of ‘chartering state’ does not even exist in international law; on the other hand, the concepts of ‘flag state’ and ‘flag state responsibility’ have been built up through hard work and international negotiations over decades, and now a small exemption, as proposed by the cites working group, threatens to set a precedent and unravel all the work that has been done up till now. we are all aware that the issue of chartering states is emerging at the speed of lightning in many of the world’s regional fisheries management organisations (rfmos). far too many countries would immediately use the argument that cites allows this exemption as an excuse to introduce the same logic in the rfmos, allowing chartering states to issue catch documents. this would totally undermine the iuu regulation. i add my voice to those asking what consequences this could have for flag-state responsibility in commercial fleets. we should not underestimate this factor. the confusion over who bears responsibility when it comes to oil spills or dumping of toxic waste is already at a maximum with ships owned in one country, flagged to another, operating in a third and landing in a fourth, with a crew and beneficial owners in a fifth. so what is the problem in cites? it is not clear to me. is it that a few countries, so-called ‘chartering states’, want to facilitate their administrative burden when they have to issue catch documents on a few hundred tonnes of endangered species of sharks that are on the cites annex 2 list? to let them do that, the world should not be ready to unravel international work on tightening the law against iuu – which actually accounts for one fifth of the world’s fish catches.
in writing. - i voted against the intergroup resolution because i found the text of it unacceptable to support. the wording and descriptions did not show any attempt at objectivity, and several facts were, i believe, missing. it is of the utmost importance that the responsibility of all parties for the peace process be highlighted. the multiparty resolution did not sufficiently emphasise the role and responsibilities of the palestinian authority. hamas' refusal to recognize israel as a country and its right to exist must also be condemned and criticized. no lasting peace can ever be achieved otherwise. the rocket attacks from the gaza strip on israeli civilians must stop. the refusal of neighbouring countries to grant citizenship to palestinian refugees and to stand up for human rights is also an obstacle to peace. the un refugee agency must play a more constructive role by revising the world-unique exception that specifically allows palestinian refugees to be inherited.

the rapporteur. - mr president! - what is it? commissioner, what is it? from day one and throughout the process of preparing this report, i have had extensive contacts with the shadow rapporteur, the public, political advisers and non-governmental organisations. i would therefore like to express my very, very warm thanks to all of you who have contributed to the presentation of this report. your suggestions and comments are now part of the whole, whether they have come in through amendments, referrals, the specially designed facebook page or orally. this is a cross-policy report, where gender equality, ecology and the environment interact. it may always be important from a number of policy perspectives, but it may be especially important in a time of financial crisis. when it comes to economic problems and their solutions, the debate often tends to be very technical. it's as if women and men don't exist, even though economics is basically about human survival on a planet with limited resources. today's economic system does not take into account the limited nature of natural resources. constant growth is almost seen as a religion. sooner or later, however, a collapse occurs, because every good or service produced requires additional extraction of natural resources, such as water, energy or metals. this is clearly affecting the ecosystems that are the foundation of our existence and well-being. we would therefore really need a green economy for all. a green or sustainable economy is a system where we preserve the productive capacity of ecosystems, the carrying capacity of the planet, while meeting basic human needs for all. the development of the economy is therefore within the limits of what nature can tolerate, ensuring a fair distribution of resources between all the people on earth - between women and men - and between generations. so this is about a sustainable economy. to create a sustainable future, we need to think beyond the market and its short-term perspective. it means that we must put the well-being of people first. to do that, we must protect the basic functions of ecosystems, because without ecosystems there is no well-being. the path to the green economy, the sustainable economy, is mainly through changes in the rich world. we are the ones who must create sustainable patterns of production and consumption. today's economic policies are not only plunging us into an environmentally dangerous ditch, but they also seem to widen the gaps in society between women and men, between rich and poor, and between north and south. the male ratio that permeates today's society must be completely changed in a sustainable society. targeted measures such as quotas are needed. transport, consumption, food, welfare and representation are areas that are strongly linked to a sustainable economy. within these areas there must be a gender perspective. the report contains a number of proposals in these areas. these are proposals that could lead to a sustainable economy where women and men have equal opportunities to determine and influence the development of society, where the planet's borders are protected and where there is social justice between all women and men.
member of the commission. − mr president, first of all i would like to thank and congratulate the rapporteur, ms satu hassi, as well as the committee on the environment, public health and food safety and the committee on transport for the excellent work carried out on this proposal. we have been waiting for it for a number of years. it was already clear in 2005 that, with business as usual, sulphur emissions from shipping would exceed those from all land-based sources in the european union by 2020. in order to reverse this trend, the commission supported the international maritime organisation (imo) in developing internationally binding standards for the quality of marine fuels, which were agreed in 2008. this was a major breakthrough. the new standards will bring very significant health and environmental benefits and will contribute to resolving problems faced by member states in complying with air quality limits. although they are highly cost-effective for the economy as a whole, the commission is aware of the potential economic impact on the shipping industry. that is why we have brought forward a set of measures to support compliance with the legislation in a staff working paper entitled the sustainable waterborne transport toolbox. this work has now culminated in the proposal on which you will vote today. the commission is satisfied with the compromise text, which aligns the directive with the international rules of annex 6 of the marpol convention in respect of sulphur standards and strengthens provisions on enforcement and monitoring. let me explain some of the very good reasons which have already been explained by ms hassi. firstly, the stricter sulphur standards agreed at the imo will now be introduced into european union legislation and hence will benefit from the european union enforcement regime. the european union legislation will introduce the global 0.5% sulphur standard in 2020, while the imo foresees a review clause with a possibility to postpone it till 2025. we support the choice made by the co-legislators as it brings certainty for investors and operators, as well as fairness across the european union. secondly, the directive will allow the use of emissions abatement technologies, such as scrubbers, or lng as alternatives to the use of low sulphur fuels. these alternative compliance methods allow operators to choose the most cost-efficient compliance option and also promote development of clean technologies. finally, the provisions on enforcement of the standards and guidelines on monitoring and reporting of fuel used by ships should result in a more effective control of compliance with the new standards. i am convinced that this directive will contribute significantly to reducing air pollution and thus will have a positive impact on people’s health. it is also a step towards a more modern and greener shipping industry. however, a number of changes introduced in the commission’s proposal during the first reading deliberations require the commission to make six declarations concerning: the revision of directive 2000/55/ec on port reception facilities for ship-generated waste and cargo residues; the use of the ‘non-opinion’ clause for the adoption of implementing acts; the use of dynamic reference to the imo on the establishment of new sulphur emission control areas; the use of implementing acts instead of delegated acts for sampling and reporting of marine fuels; the request to the commission to develop new measures supporting the implementation of the directive by the end of 2012; and finally the ongoing work to review the eu thematic strategy on air pollution to assess the costs and benefits of additional measures to reduce air pollution from shipping, including the impact of applying a maximum fuel sulphur standard of 0.1% in territorial waters. we will of course pass the full text of these declarations to the plenary services to be recorded in the minutes. the commission firmly believes that the outcome of the codecision process is a good step forward and it accepts the compromise reached at first reading. so, once again, congratulations on the very good work. it was not an easy compromise but it was very well done.
for the ppe group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. it is with mixed feelings that i stand here today, because the decision we have to vote on, namely the implementation of the imo sulphur agreement, is something that we have had very little opportunity to influence. i think it's far from brilliant. it is welcome that the sulphur issue is finally being addressed and that we have a global solution, because it is a major health problem. as the commissioner pointed out, sulphur emissions from shipping will soon exceed those from land. anyone who's seen bunker oil knows what i mean. bunker oil is so dirty that in many cases it needs to be heated up to even be able to enter the engine and burn it. but as i said, the way in which this decision has been taken is far from brilliant, indeed, quite unreasonable. europe is being divided into two parts. the baltic sea, the north sea and the english channel, which already have the strictest regulations in the world, are being given even stricter rules, while not doing nearly as much in other parts of europe. it's as if greek lives are worth less than, say, danish ones. in the european parliament's handling of the matter we have tried to correct this absurdity and to ensure that we get tougher demands everywhere at the same time. we reached a small part of the road but far from enough. this decision does not give us equal opportunities throughout europe. this is going to be incredibly expensive, multi-billion dollar. it will be reflected in already strained state budgets. i saw yesterday how industries announced that they were moving. if that's the case, we haven't won anything. but the countries themselves have asked for this. the countries most affected have asked for this. so it is up to us to implement this decision. i think that order is unreasonable. we, who are obliged to legislate on this matter, should of course have been involved from the outset in the discussion of what this imo decision would have looked like. we cannot stand here and say that we would have reacted five minutes before noon when the whole debate on the consequences of the decision was five minutes after noon. if i could have stopped that part, i would have, but unfortunately, i wasn't offered that opportunity.
the s&d group is in favour of the proposal. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. four years ago, therefore, the global decision was taken to create this stricter sulphur directive. all eu governments took part in that decision. our task has been to translate this into eu law. we have had little opportunity to change the content, but i think we have succeeded on a few points. but that does not mean that you can be satisfied with the result. we can be satisfied with the environmental and health aspects, at least in the northern half of europe, because the requirements mean that we are taking a step towards treating our polluted seas, our seas which are in danger of becoming extinct, and therefore us humans, in a better way. we should be aware that the requirements for emissions from land transport are a hundred times higher than those for maritime transport. we can't have it like that. the problem, however, is that we are putting enormous pressure on the north of europe and no additional pressure on the south of europe, at least not significantly. we are creating two europe. that is not possible where there is to be a single internal market. i hope that our proposal has now made up some of the differences, and we must continue to work on this.
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to thank the rapporteur for her excellent work. the shipping industry would like to claim that shipping is an environmentally friendly mode of transport. i think it is wrong to "grumble" that we ask them to either stop burning waste - which is what they have in mind now, that's what you can compare bunker fuel to - or that when they burn this waste they actually at least clean it. these are reasonable demands that will save 50,000 lives. the fact that there is a tightening of the rules in the baltic sea is quite natural, since it is an extremely sensitive sea. boiled water is not something that organisms are perfectly adapted to. the organisms are sensitive. it makes sense to have stricter rules there. nor can everyone install scrubbers and change vessels at the same time. it's good to have a few people ahead of us. the cost of this is greatly exaggerated. they correspond to about one fifth of a eurocent in dividends to the companies that have to invest in it. we can afford it. but we cannot afford the alternative of not doing so.
