negotiations on a free trade agreement with the eu. we agree that there is reason to remain vigilant on the eurasian customs union, but we cannot automatically assume it is incompatible with russia’s wto obligations. our group filed a number of amendments for which i would like to ask your support. in particular we would like to rethink the wisdom of paragraph 11, last indent, regarding pre-establishment investment protection and to request support for our amendment 8. russia has for a long time been the only country with a reasonably well-developed information and communications infrastructure that has not been subject to the trips agreement requirements. this has led to interesting developments in the field of content distribution in russia, not only in illegal downloading – a topic which we discuss with far too much aggression too frequently in our house – but also in distribution schemes that are, or were at the time of their emergence, legal under the russian system. at the same time the russian authorities appeared to have no regard for freedom of communication or freedom of information flows. at least once during my time of political involvement, russian authorities have casually shut down legal online distribution services at the request of foreign entities to accommodate the advancement of world trade organisation discussions. it would be a shame if the trips implementation chosen by russia turned out to hinder rather than foster the previously-mentioned spirit of solutions for online distribution for the future. and there is good reason for the european union to keep a close watch on the implementation of the trips obligations in russia. they establish a basis for economic interaction which depends completely on an administrative right, a monopoly granted by the government and its legal institutions to private parties. are we confident that the russian administration is consistent enough to provide such a basis? in the field of copyright in particular, the trips agreement and the present interpretation adopted by the disputes settlement body of the berne three-step test should cause us to feel concern for how future implementation of russian copyright will relate to freedom of speech and freedom of expression. already at european level we are suffering from inconsistencies with the application of notice and take-down orders. (the president requested the speaker to speak more slowly) one of the concerns raised in the european union by many stakeholders is that notice and take-down requests risk imposing arbitrary restrictions on freedom of speech and freedom of expression in the member states. similar objections have been raised in spain and france in the implementation of laws that grant rightholders the power to request the take-down of a web page through administrative requests rather than judicial process. the trips agreement does not establish any boundaries for enforcement. in theory it can be extended as far and as arbitrarily as a signatory party wants it to be extended. therefore we have to ask ourselves whether or not the situation in terms of freedom of speech and freedom of expression will be affected by the implementation of trips in russia. given our experiences in europe, we need to keep a close eye on this. we will also have the opportunity to revisit the question of the monitoring of communications. the situation in many european jurisdictions with the data retention directive and in terms of intermediary liability for intellectual property rights infringements is uncertain. also in russia there are extensive obligations on telecommunications providers and service providers to monitor the communications of their users. we have every reason to be vigilant so that the situation in russia does not deteriorate with respect to the monitoring of communications. in terms of access control and allegedly illegal content on line, the issue of blocking access to specific content has already been debated many times and by many people in the member states and here in parliament, but these proposals also exist in russia, again with the higher risk of arbitrary application and again with a broader scope than we would normally find in europe. if the european union wishes to see the implementation of human rights, freedom of speech, free journalism and a free press in russia, it will be important for us to establish – firstly for ourselves – open norms for free communication.
madam president, i want to share with you a story about a user comment on youtube that i found a few months ago. it says that the user is against pirated videos but really liked the song in question, and so offers ‘an apology for the copyright’. that perfectly encapsulates what is wrong with the copyright system – when users apologise for interacting with culture in a way that they find most natural to them. the song in question is a disco remix of a popular song made by gucci vump, a dj who carries the name of a luxury bag. in its unmixed form, the song in question has a music video clearly inspired by the hbo series game of thrones, which in turn is based on the very famous fantasy books by an author called george r.r. martin. i was struck, when i found this youtube video, by the user’s comments on his own behaviour and by how radically copyright contrasts with what he wants to do, but also by the many turns and twists that culture takes in our society to find its way into our lives and enrich our day-to-day existence.
madam president, i would like to thank you. mr commissioner, i would like to thank you for your reply. the change of power in georgia has taken place following democratic, fair and free elections, as recognised by the osce and the council of europe. this is a historic step for georgia, but more such transfers of power are needed before we can say with certainty that democracy and free elections have come to georgia to stay. during the transitional period, all political parties must demonstrate their ability to work together and take responsibility for georgia's future and its european path. it is cooperation and the ability to compromise that will determine whether the period of constitutional coexistence will be successful. in this sense, both mr saakashvili's quick acknowledgement that his party was defeated and mr ivanishvili's withdrawal of the demand for saakashvili's resignation give me hope that this cooperation and this willingness to compromise exist. i urge both gentlemen to work together for the good of georgia. it is also gratifying that the new government has expressed its willingness to continue on the european path and that mr ivanishvili intends to make his first trip to brussels. it is equally important to continue cooperation with nato and to continue euro-atlantic integration. the eu intends to further develop its relations with georgia. it is good for georgia and it is good for the eu.
mr president, i would like to thank you. a while ago, i was visiting my colleague and friend moso koa from the sam rainsy party in cambodia. what i saw around the country was horrifying, and the images i may carry with me forever. countless families have been victims of the confiscation of their homes and land. the government and big business have seized the land that families have worked for generations. those who have been affected have received no compensation but have been left to fend for themselves, without justice and without help. the eu's 'everything but arms' programme is a good initiative in principle, but it has actually had very negative effects on sugar and some other agricultural products, because the giant sugar companies have taken over and are now farming agricultural land. countless villages have been razed to the ground and thousands of families have been made homeless to make way for sugar plantations that are profiting from the lucrative agreement with the eu. i therefore welcome the fact that the agreement contains a call to suspend this, and appeal to cambodia to allow opposition leader sam rainsy to return to his country before the elections.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'commercial vehicle' to cover vehicles with a maximum mass exceeding 10 tonnes. we did so because we see a need for updated and improved legislation in this respect, in particular through more transparency and openness. there are, however, three areas where we remain sceptical. the first concerns competition between clearing houses at the close of trading. the proposal as it stands now risks restricting competition, which would be detrimental to the functioning of the market and to individual investors. the second concerns trading in commodity-related instruments, where several restrictions are introduced which risk leading to a deterioration in price formation. the third and final point concerns high-frequency trading, where the current wording of the proposal is aimed at regulating technological developments rather than the possible element of market abuse that may arise in the wake of these developments. we believe that technological progress should be encouraged, while abuses and irregularities should be tackled with increased supervision.
vice-president of the commission. − mr president, i did not expect any less from parliament, which has expressed itself very clearly on this issue. i must say that i am very shocked. you could pick any issue over which to have an institutional fight, but do not pick children. even if only one child’s life is put in danger because of this institutional fight, that is one too many. i am aware that the lives of children are at stake. we should finally stop playing games. that is the message i have for the member states, and i hope that they have heard the message from the european parliament. thank you for helping me to fight on this issue.
vice-president of the commission. − mr president, i think that we are all aware of the threat posed to public safety by terrorists. i do not need to recall the loss of life caused by indiscriminate attacks in recent years. this is a phenomenon which needs to be tackled from a number of angles. today we are dealing with one aspect: how to make it more difficult for violent extremists to obtain the ingredients for home-made explosives. the commission proposed this regulation two years ago. the aim was to put in place harmonised rules, limiting the access of the general public to certain explosive precursors that could be used in the illicit manufacture of explosives. these rules cover the making available of these substances to the public, as well as their possession and use. by acting at eu level, we can guarantee a harmonised approach and create a level playing field for retailers and for the public. i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr mulder, and the successive hungarian, polish, danish and cyprus presidencies for their excellent cooperation in this field. the commission welcomes the agreement reached by co-legislators. the text they have agreed upon will significantly improve our common security and bring tangible european added value. however, there is one institutional point of continued disagreement relating to the preparation of delegated acts, and on this point i would like to make the following declaration on behalf of the commission. the commission takes note of the agreement reached by co-legislators on article 91, as well as the unanimity in council; however, it recalls that article 290 is to be interpreted as meaning that the commission is autonomous in the preparation and adoption of delegated acts. the common understanding agreed between the three institutions is a reflection of that interpretation. accordingly, reference to consultation of experts should be contained in a recital, not in the enacting terms. the commission regrets that this principle was not respected and underlines that the present case cannot constitute a precedent. i hope, however, that tomorrow there will be a positive vote, so that the council can formally adopt the legislation before the end of the year. the commission is ready to do its part, notably by issuing guidelines on implementation before the new rules are applied, which means 18 months after the regulation enters into force.
on behalf of the ppe group. – mr president, in the peaceful summer of 2011, we were all shocked to learn that in norway – on an island where young people had been gathering – a massacre had been committed, and that the heart of oslo was also bombed out. according to europol, one of the reasons why we were able to find the perpetrator was that he had been buying a lot of the chemical materials that can be used to make explosives at home. that is why i welcome our finally coming together in the european union, in the member states, and hopefully also in this house tomorrow on this very good proposal from the commission – thank you, commissioner reding – on harmonising the existing rules to prevent such terrible accidents and incidents from happening again. terrorism and criminality are without borders – are cross-border – while our rules are fragmented and divergent. what is the next step forward? firstly, at least in most of the member states, the public will require a licence to be able to buy these explosives – these potential explosives – in a shop or with wholesalers. secondly, these chemical substances should not be sold above a certain concentration or a certain threshold that would make them suitable for making home-made explosives. thirdly, this legislation will ensure traceability, which means that economic operators will have to report when these materials have been bought in large quantities. i think we also have a good system for protecting data when these transactions are reported, and it is up to member states to define what kind of penalties will exist. this is a first step, and in three years’ time the commission will have to report back on whether there is a need to go further, and whether there is a need to harmonise further. i would invite the commission to monitor and follow this very closely to see whether the rules that we are now approving are adequate and sufficient to ensure the safety and security of our citizens. i think we have struck the right balance between safety and security, which was our primary concern, without adding red tape for companies, for farmers, or for hotels with swimming pools which need to be cleaned with chemicals. i also think that we have struck a balance between reporting and data protection. i would also like to welcome and thank the shadow rapporteur of the epp group, ms véronique mathieu, who is at this time unfortunately attending a very important meeting on criminality in her capacity as coordinator. i would however like to say that she very much enjoyed the cooperation with the rapporteur. we in the epp group believe that she has gone the extra mile to show flexibility and to come together with others on what is an extremely important piece of legislation to prevent criminality and terrorism and to protect our citizens in europe.
mr president, i would like to highlight one particular article in this report – article 32. it says that it is likely that more and more companies in europe will be dependent on having access to credit card payments and it urges the commission to come up with objective rules to say under what circumstances the big payment providers – visa, mastercard and paypal – may reject a business. this came up in the context of the wikileaks affair, when the same three big payment services decided to close down a whistleblowers’ website based in iceland – inside the european economic area – because of political pressure from the us. but we also saw only last week that there has been quite a lot of talk in the media about visa, mastercard and paypal deciding to close down a small shop that was selling horror movies simply because they do not like horror movies. i understand that they have now since changed their opinion on that, but it illustrates the problem and i hope the commission will look seriously at this.

mr president, this debate is not really about money. the budget corresponds to only about 2% of european public expenditure; it has been falling as a proportion of the member states’ economies for years and contrary to what mr callanan and mr farage believe, the main criticism from the court of auditors is not against the european institutions but on how eu money is handled by member states. the member states who fight for cuts are the strongest ones; the ones who can pay if they feel that the union is worth defending. no, it is not about money; this is a fight over the european model and the future of europe. the reason why we social democrats, together with a huge majority of parliament, defend the budget even in times of crisis is that the budget is the most significant expression of our fundamental policies. it is about european and even global solidarity. it is about creating a european added value and the basic idea that the growth we are striving for is not just any growth but is based on the principle of economic, social and environmental sustainability, is about green jobs and smart and inclusive growth. these are fundamental things which will not be funded without the european union. let me mention just a few examples. who will fund the completion of the single market including a modern cross-border transport energy and digital infrastructure if not the eu budget? who will start breaking the vicious circle of austerity if we do not do it together? who will provide the contribution to the innovative financial instruments including project bonds for funding investments? i mean, with all the restrictions on national budgets, on banks and financial institutions, only the european union can do it. how can we respect our commitment to funding the millennium development goals if not through the eu budget? it is obvious that member states under pressure will not even fulfil their commitments to allocate 0.7 % of gni to development aid. we need the european union for that. against the background of new devastating research on the effects of climate change no member state is big enough to mitigate climate change on its own. we must do it together and the european union is the major global actor in this field. even if some of us do not like common agricultural policies we must ask: who will finance the greening of agriculture and the greening of fisheries and the more sustainable development of those policies if we do not do it together? so this is not about money; it is about giving the european union the chance to finally realise the role we all agreed on in the lisbon treaty.

in writing. - as regards the report by the committee on industry on shale gas and oil, i share the view that policy-makers should have access to more comprehensive scientific data (e.g. carbon footprint data) in order to make well-informed choices. i understand that there is an economic potential for shale gas extraction and that the emphasis is therefore on adapting systems, investments, regulations, legislation, etc., but not at the high human and environmental cost.
mr president, i would like to thank you. at the cancún climate summit, the 1.5°c global temperature goal was described as the limit that the earth could tolerate without too dramatic consequences. in durban, it was changed to the 2 degree target, and earlier this week, the forecast came out that today's climate policies are leading us to 4 degrees of warming, which is a very scary figure. the question that a lot of people ask me is: is it too late already? what measures and decisions can break such a strong negative trend? is that even possible? these are the questions we are trying to answer. what we also need to do in doha is to ensure that the eu can act in unison, as many here have already pointed out, but also to think about how to get china and the united states back seriously to the joint negotiating table. we can hope that the new governments and leaders in the united states and china are prepared to take new steps, but it really depends on us. we did good last time, but we have to do even better. we must contribute to concrete results and to the people's ability to continue to hope.
mr president, i would like to thank you. despite the economic crisis, we in the epp also support enlargement. there are a few important points that we are making in the report. the most important thing is that we emphasize our strong support for the enlargement process, not least at a time when less democratic and less european forces are trying to convince us that europe is about something else than freedom, peace and security. it is also important that we recognise that enlargement is good for the candidate countries and for europe. it is a good framework within which to discuss our political visions and the fulfilment of the criteria by the candidate countries, which is so important for the credibility of the enlargement process. the new serbian government, for example, has taken chapter 23 seriously and has done a very good job in the fight against corruption and organised crime. however, some elements of the structures of the previous government still have influence and sometimes make this work difficult. so we must be alert and help when and where needed. like my colleague eduard kukan, i too think that this report places a little too much emphasis on things like the social dimension and the capacity of the eu to integrate. i would have liked to have had a clearer definition of the visions and security aspects of enlargement. finally, i would like to thank you, commissioner, for your personal commitment to these issues. your commitment is so much more than just your work. you're doing a great job, and i want to thank you for that.
rapporteur. − mr president, as the rapporteur on the international dimension of the common fisheries policy reform, i am quite proud today to say that ‘we have actually done it’! in the almost unanimous vote in the committee on fisheries, colleagues supported, and to a large extent improved, my draft report, so that it now clearly sets out the future principles for the eu external fishing fleet and how it can be sustainable in all its activities. it shows how the eu can become a leader by setting an example of responsible behaviour by a long-distance fishing nation, worldwide. what we have achieved is quite remarkable. we reached a consensus that the eu should negotiate a fisheries agreement only where there is a clearly and scientifically-proven surplus of fish that is not needed by local fishermen or people in the region. of course, the fish stocks must also be in good condition. we agreed that the same rules – for instance, on discarding fish – should apply to eu vessels whether they are operating inside or outside the union. we also agreed to introduce a human rights clause into fisheries agreements so that, if there is a serious violation of human rights in a third country, the protocols shall be suspended. we stated the need to decouple access fees from the money for so-called sectoral support in a clear way, so that poor countries should not be tempted to sell more fish in order to get more development aid. widespread support was also shown for the concept of regional agreements. the eu should not use its strength to negotiate agreements with countries in developing parts of the world one by one, but rather should encourage regional cooperation, which would lead to better management of fishery resources and transparency on total catches. we also recognise that the eu and its member states have responsibilities in relation to the many private operators owned by eu interests, but often flying other flags, that are fishing all over the world. the committee also recognised our heavy responsibility as the greatest fish consumer market in the world, importing and eating 11% of all the world’s catches in terms of volume. i do not want to hide the fact that there is some controversial content in the report and that the biggest group in parliament is still trying to get these things out of tomorrow’s vote – the anti-flag hopping clause, for example, the exclusivity clause and the statement that vessel owners should pay a considerable market-based share of the access fees. i regret this and i really hope that those – even in the epp group – who want to see the eu leading by example on issues relating to environmental sustainability, fair trade and respect for human rights, will not try to delete the paragraphs in question. one paragraph that i would like to emphasise is paragraph 7, which is about greater coordination inside the commission and among member states. that is really necessary if we are to achieve policy coherence on development, in accordance with article 208 of the treaty on the functioning of the european union. we cannot have the eu negotiating a fisheries agreement and using development aid and trade agreements as a means of pressure to push through access to fishing grounds in developing countries. i believe we have a great report here that can not only change the impact of the eu on the world’s oceans, but could also change how all distance-fishing nations behave. if we can show that the eu is willing to change, poor countries will have an easier time stipulating conditions for other distance-fishing nations. with this report we have started a race to the top in terms of good standards. i want to thank all the shadow rapporteurs, the assistants and advisers who helped with the report. we have the promise here of a great success.
the committee on the environment, public health and food safety has tabled an opinion. - mr president! - what is it? the salmon is endangered and the main threat to the salmon is fishing and hydropower. it's not the seals and the cormorants that eat the majority of the salmon. this is important and i am pleased that the committee on fisheries has approved many of the proposals of the committee on the environment, particularly with regard to the 80% reproductive capacity target and to stricter and clearer regulation of recreational, domestic and sport fisheries, as these account for a significant proportion of catches. in addition, it is good that we have improved reporting systems in general, and what i lack in the report of the committee on fisheries is what we approved in the committee on the environment, namely to stop sea fishing, because it is not selective. you may catch the few necessary wild salmon to return to the areas where they must breed, namely endangered fairies. i welcome the proposal, by the way. this could be the beginning of the salmon's recovery, and it's not just good for the environment. it's an economic benefit to many interests in society and it creates thousands of jobs. more fish means more jobs and more money.
mr president, i would like to thank you. as a resident of northern sweden, i hope that with tomorrow's decision we will take important steps to ensure that the reproductive capacity of wild salmon in the baltic sea is improved, but there is certainly much more to be done in the future. i actually asked for the floor to ask about shark fins. when i was new to the committee on fisheries in february, i was caught up in a discussion about the shark fin and i was surprised because i think it is 10 years since it was banned. if it was banned 10 years ago and it's still going on, we have to do something. i am very pleased that with tomorrow's decision we will hopefully close the loopholes in the current rules. fishing rules must be adapted to the objective, easy to implement on the water and equally easy to control in port. in this way we will effectively stop the misconduct without making it worse.
rapporteur. − mr president, thank you to all the colleagues who have commented on and supported the report on the external dimension of the common fisheries policy. we really appreciate the commission’s original proposal and we also appreciate that the commission welcomes the additional dimension that we have added to the external communication – the inclusion of the issues of trade, private agreements and private operators from the eu that are actually fishing outside of the eu waters. they should somehow also be included, in our view, in the external dimension of the cfp. the eu is now very dependent on imports of fish. we are importing 60%, but the 40% that is counted as ‘domestic fisheries’ is actually 23% of the value of that, through the fisheries partnerships agreements. so this is really not sustainable. what we are doing is not sustainable; we are making ourselves more and more dependent on fish from foreign seas, and this is not how the eu should proceed. if we want to take responsibility for our own consumption of fish, we must first rebuild european stocks. we should therefore take the reform of the cfp really seriously and rebuild all the european stocks to levels above those capable of producing msy. secondly, in our fisheries partnerships agreements we take development cooperation seriously and help developing countries to develop their own fisheries industry so that they can support their own populations and perhaps export some of the fish to us. that is the way to go.
mr president, i would like to thank you. it feels a little odd to be debating support for the solar cell industry. it is something we usually discuss, but then it is that europe should give more support to the development and research of solar cell production in europe. now we are discussing criticism of china for allegedly giving too much support to its industry. if we decide to impose these anti-dumping duties, they could be the biggest anti-dumping duties we have ever imposed. therefore, it is important to be very careful and very thorough. there are very large values at stake. it is not just european solar production that is at stake, but also trade relations with china and our energy and climate policies. because these are not the only anti-dumping duties we have on renewable energy. we already have anti-dumping duties on biodiesel, ethanol and even the plastics used to make wind turbines. i therefore call on the commission to make the overall assessment it is required to make in this inquiry, to take the community interest seriously and to examine the implications of this also for our energy and climate policy.
in writing. - i voted in favour of the gróbaczyk report on a management plan for salmon in the baltic sea, as it contains very important components which will improve the state of wild salmon, such as phasing out farmed salmon discharges, as well as reporting requirements also for domestic and recreational fisheries. however, we failed to find political support for phasing out all fishing for salmon in the high seas and to introduce a common minimum size for salmon and herring, which would have reduced the risks of misreporting and fraud. nevertheless, i think that the gróbaczyk report is a very big step forward compared to the present situation.

in writing. the european parliament has already adopted a resolution on the situation in the former yugoslavia. we would like to stress, however, that this should not affect the freedom of member states to form military alliances. we would have liked to have seen a paper that took up the wording of article 42 (7) of the eu treaty that the eu's solidarity clause and the mutual defence clause should not affect the specific nature of the security and defence policy of certain member states. we do not support the writings on the need for the gradual formulation of a common defence policy aimed at a common defence which would strengthen the european identity and the strategic autonomy of the eu and harmonize the eu's defence policy.
in writing. - i voted against joão ferreira's own-initiative report on small-scale fishing, although i broadly agree with the content and the importance of giving a high degree of consideration and priority to small-scale fishing in the forthcoming reform of the cfp. however, i do not think that the right way to favour small-scale fishermen is to provide financial compensation for increased fuel prices, to compensate fishermen for not fishing during periods when fish are not allowed to fish, or to provide aid for the storage of fish on land. the best way to support small-scale fishermen is to give them priority access to fishery resources, to support the processing of their own fishery products where appropriate and, above all, to ensure that stocks grow so that they can make a reasonable profit from their fishing.

for the alde group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. there's really only one word that captures the feeling i'm carrying inside me today, and that's the word finally. finally, we have succeeded in creating what the eu has failed to do for almost four decades, namely a common european patent, despite the fact that we all knew how much good such a patent could do for individuals, inventors, innovators, universities and colleges, smes, entrepreneurs, etc. it's been a long and difficult journey. in the end, closer cooperation between 25 of the 27 member states was needed to reach an agreement. but i would also like to stress today that the door is wide open for spain and italy, which have so far chosen to remain outside, and that these countries are welcome as soon as they consider it desirable. i look forward to their choosing the common path in the future. today i would like to express my special thanks to commissioner barnier for his and the commission's tireless work on this proposal. i am glad, together with the commissioner, that he is the one who brought this matter to the attention of the house. i would also like to thank the three rapporteurs of the european parliament, mr rapkay, mr lehne and mr baldassarre, who have led the work of this parliament in an efficient and very constructive way, and i am glad to have been part of that. it has always been the liberal group's natural policy to promote the emergence of a single european patent, providing uniform protection combined with a single european patent court for dispute settlement. in the future, it will no longer be necessary to spend more than ten times as much to protect an invention in europe as in the united states. an individual inventor or a small enterprise should not have to defend his patent against infringement in parallel legal proceedings in several european countries at the same time, but in a single european patent court with common european procedural rules. in this process, my aim has been to ensure that we maintain the balance of the european patent system while creating a system without unnecessary administrative burdens, and we have succeeded well. this is a historic day. we have laid a cornerstone in strengthening the eu's single market to enable growth and prosperity in our member states in the days ahead. this is extremely important because we must dare to see europe beyond the crisis. i'm not saying that patents are the answer, but it's one of many tools that will ensure prosperity and growth in the future.
mr president, the green group, including the pirate party, would have wanted a unitary patent – and we still want a unitary patent – if by this we mean a proper unitary patent integrated into the legal and political structures of the eu. unfortunately, what we see and what we have here on the table – and for which there appears to be a majority in favour – is the very opposite of that. this agreement means that parliament will abdicate all its political powers over the future of the patent system to an intergovernmental organisation that is outside the eu. it means that the court system, the legal system of this new patent, will be outside the proper eu legal system. this is a bad thing for innovation, a bad thing for lots of things in europe. so why does it matter? as has been mentioned, we have for several decades had the european patent office which is an international organisation outside the eu, outside the control of the eu, outside the control of parliament. we can see what this has led to. the treaty that is the foundation of the work of the european patent office, the european patent convention (epc), explicitly says that you cannot patent software, you cannot patent life, etc. we know that the european patent office does not care about this. it grants those patents anyway, using various legal formulas. for software, for instance, they say, ‘oh no, we would never allow patents on software as such, we would only allow patents on software that runs on computers’. this is obviously a rubbish argument but that is how they work. this would have been an opportunity to bring the patent system into line, to get it under judicial and political control, but we have missed that opportunity by simply abdicating and giving more power to this organisation outside the law. i would really urge colleagues to reconsider this because, yes, we do have different opinions about the patent system, but there is one thing that we do actually agree on: we agree that how the patent system works is important for the european economy. we all agree that a good patent system is good for the economy, it is good for innovation, it is something that we want, whereas a bad system is harmful and quite bad. since we agree on this, it makes no sense at all to abdicate power over this and to give away one of the few things that we, as politicians, can actually do through legislation. i urge all colleagues, please, to support the green amendments and let us start working towards a real unitary patent.
madam president, i would like to thank you. it is good that the new eu patent finally seems to be coming into place. this is a good reform. the innovation climate is improved by making the patent easier and cheaper to manage. better conditions for innovative european companies will strengthen them in the face of increasingly tough global competition. this in turn is important for europe to boost both growth and job creation. in fact, the eu patent is one of the best growth news in a long time. there will be some objections to the deal now on the table. many of them are fraudulent, and some of them are legitimate. my view is that the agreement is not perfect, but it is nevertheless a good compromise, given the circumstances. the new rules are balanced. the new patent system will be fully operational. so it's only natural for me to say yes to the deal. it is indeed high time that we went from words to action in the field of the european patent.
in writing. − completing the digital single market is a relevant own-initiative report which raises many important topics for the development of digital services for european citizens. it forgets, however, to raise one of the most outstanding issues for knowledge services today: the lack of flexibility in copyright law. parliament must start requesting a more balanced approach to access to knowledge from the commission. our single-minded focus on enforcement of copyright, without questioning what is actually enforced, ultimately does much damage to our communities, our social and cultural cohesion, as well as our economic development. therefore, i rejected this report.
in writing. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of electronic data interchange. in times of crisis, the single market is the highway to recovery, growth and more jobs. this is even more true online, where there is a huge untapped potential. e-commerce also increases access to goods and services in rural areas and is an important complement to physical stores. i am committed to breaking down barriers to e-commerce and to building trust in online commerce across borders. different rules make it harder for both small businesses and consumers. for example, problems arise with purchases that do not go through when you buy from a website in another eu country. such discrimination should no longer exist in our common market. i therefore welcome the fact that the report promotes the free movement of goods and services and simple, common rules. it is important to create safer payments, improve deliveries by opening up to more competition, simplify administrative rules, increase transparency and legal certainty to create more confidence in online shopping across europe and at the same time invest in broadband for all. i am particularly pleased that my proposal for the commission to apply an 'e-commerce test' was widely supported, to ensure that single market rules do not create unnecessary barriers to e-commerce.

in writing. - i welcome the fact that, after many years of negotiations, we have finally reached an agreement on unitary european patent protection and a patent court. it is an important step towards boosting the eu's competitiveness and opening up more innovation. with a single european patent as a simple 'one-stop-shop', we reduce costs and red tape, create more legal certainty and opportunities for businesses, universities and inventors, and contribute to a better functioning internal market with less fragmentation. it is important that the agreement also contains specific measures to simplify the application of patents for smes. in times of crisis, this is a concrete tool to make the eu more competitive, also on the global market.
in writing. − the unitary patent protection, as created, does not create a unitary patent protection. interpretation of the law will still be highly dependent on member states. the proposed system maintains the problems faced by small and medium-sized enterprises and it does not address the outstanding issue of functional claims in patent applications – claims which apply to a problem, rather than a solution. the limitations and exceptions to the law are few and badly-worded, a mistake at a time of more dependence on free information and where patent litigation proliferates, making clarity in patents of the essence. i would have hoped that the parliament could have let this debate mature further before taking a final decision.
in writing. − the jurisdictional problem in europe for patent law is a very serious one which creates much legal uncertainty and space for legal manoeuvring, particularly by multinational companies. unfortunately, an own-initiative report has added to a pile of legislative resolutions which do not appropriately tackle this issue and cannot remedy these problems. it is also doubtful that the issues raised in this report would fix the problems we have seen in, for instance, mobile phone patent disputes. therefore i have rejected this own-initiative report.
in writing. − i have already criticised this agreement in this plenary room in the spring. it is badly balanced in how it restricts information flows through its intellectual property rights chapters and maximalist provisions. it is better for the european union and many of its citizens if we, in our international trade relations, consider also the trade-restricting effects that arise from zealous intellectual property rights enforcement. we must also consider the social and cultural damage we are doing to institutionally weaker jurisdictions by forcing on them an ipr enforcement system which they may not adequately be able to balance against important rights such as freedom of speech and right to education. i am disappointed that parliament could not find it in their hearts to postpone this vote, and i have rejected the agreement.

mr president, i would like to thank you. since sweden joined the eu in 1995, we have been waiting for shorter transport times. eight hours is the big campaign. however, this does not preclude demands for better conditions during these hours and for better implementation of the directive. this is not contradictory, but eight hours facilitates implementation. eight hours, as i said, is not a scientific limit. many animals do not feel good about being put in an unfamiliar situation, and it starts the first minute of transport. the length of time one accepts a transport is therefore at least as much a political and ethical question of how much suffering one accepts as a scientific question of hormone levels and stress hormones. therefore, it is appropriate to have a clear requirement. besides, we've waited long enough. it is important that the commission presents a proposal as soon as possible before the end of this mandate so that we can improve all the elements of the proposal and not just the transport time. the animals are also too crowded. how many of you would share your double bed with four cows?
i would like to thank the rapporteur for his report. in fact, it is not wise that the human race can be so totally shut off from feeling empathy and compassion for other creatures. i sometimes wonder how you can look through the fingers of someone who is suffering and enduring torture and cruel conditions. to allow animals to travel unreasonably long distances for hours on end in grotesquely small spaces is insane! if you do not want to introduce animal transport for a maximum of eight hours, you should do what carl schlyter said, try sleeping with four cows in a double bed. for that is the case with cows, and the same appalling conditions exist for other animals. no one can do everything, but together we can do a little. let's set a limit of eight hours max. even that is a long time. we should at least be able to agree that it should not be more than eight hours of transport. don't put money before animal welfare!
mr president, i would like to thank you. this year's report on fundamental rights has been more controversial than in previous years. given the impact of the economic crisis on europe and the violations of fundamental rights, the situation is more serious than it has been for a long time. despite the hard work of the rapporteur and the genuine desire of other political groups to produce a joint report, the epp has chosen to put forward an alternative which is a totally watered-down text, which is absolutely not in line with the objectives of the eu. it is appalling how different our views are here in the european parliament on what fundamental rights are! there are many reasons to vote for the broad joint report. the main reason is that discrimination against lgbt people, abuse of refugees and persecution of roma will not stop until we openly dare to acknowledge that these things are happening in the eu today and take political action to stop them. the european parliament has a unique opportunity to do just that in this report. another important issue that the epp report does not address is the right to sexual and reproductive health for all european women. after the shameful and tragic death of savita halappanavar in ireland, it is high time that we as members of the european parliament once again stress that women's right to their own body is a fundamental right. yesterday the eu received the nobel peace prize, but now it seems that the european parliament is unable to adopt a joint report that respects the fundamental rights of europe. it's a disgrace!
blue-card question. – madam president, i am very much in favour of sweden joining the banking union. i hope that the swedish government, the prime minister and the minister of finance will do that and will announce that. it is complicated in my country. but i would like to ask you, mr giegold: what do you think will happen if countries like sweden, denmark and poland are outside the banking union? what will the consequences for stability in europe be?
madam president, this monday the european union received the nobel prize for having united europe. i belong to those who think that this is a miraculous development that we have seen. the last 50 years, and not least the last 20 years – when we have unified europe with a european union of 27 members, soon to be 28 – have been a miracle. but i must say that we have a reason to be concerned that, in exactly the same week, we are now starting a discussion about how to develop the institutions in ways that risk dividing us. we are using the words ‘ins and outs’, ‘pluses and minuses’, ‘non-euro and euro’, and ‘north and south’. i think that, if history is to remember these days, we need to give priority to keeping the union together, for political as well as economic reasons, because the only way out of the crisis is to have a strong european union, using the opportunities of being the world’s biggest economy and the world’s biggest market, and being able to do all the things we need to do. that is a responsibility for the future, and we should underline that at the meeting this week.
madam president, i would like to thank you. perhaps the most important decision of this parliamentary term will be taken in the next few days on the establishment of a banking union. it is my hope that a solution can be found and that a compromise can be reached, involving as many countries as possible. i really hope that my own country, sweden, will choose to join, and i sincerely hope that gunnar hökmark shares this view. it is in sweden's interest to join the banking union. my appeal to prime minister reinfeldt and finance minister borg is therefore very clear: say yes to the banking union! it is good for sweden and also for europe.
mr president, for the s&d group it has been a condition for approving the package on amending budget 6 and the 2013 budget that we get this upgraded joint statement, with the signatures of the presidents of the institutions, in order to verify the need for an amending budget early in 2013 to get the necessary additional resources to pay the outstanding bills for 2012. as we now have heard this statement from president schulz and have received the necessary signatures, i hereby withdraw all our eleven budgetary amendments – that is, the amendments on figures – while we of course keep our amendment on the resolution.
the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the euro as a currency in the euro area. in view of the economic situation in the member states, we believe that this should be done within the framework of a budget not exceeding the current level. we believe that the eu budget must be given sufficient resources to finance commitments already made. at the same time, we would like to stress that the eu must first of all adopt a restrictive approach and provide these resources by redistributing them within the existing budget. the agricultural budget should be cut in favour of those areas which are currently facing payment problems, i.e. the social fund, research programmes and erasmus. the negotiating compromise we have reached today contains on the one hand the means to pay outstanding bills, to invest in growth, jobs and solidarity, but on the other hand it lacks sufficient ambition for savings and reform. we have therefore decided to abstain in the final vote. we would like to stress that a 'no' to the compromise would have meant that the eu would not have been able to pay its 2012 bills and that no 2013 budget would have been in place on time. we believe that in this difficult economic situation it would be too much to contribute to a budgetary crisis. finally, we believe it is important to make the eu's administrative expenditure more efficient and to reduce it, and we welcome the fact that the budgets of all the eu institutions except the eeas are kept below the rate of inflation. in this context, we also welcome the savings made in the travel allowances of members of the european parliament and the reduction in the appropriations for the 'house of european history'. however, work on further savings must continue.

in writing. - we voted today in favour of seven reports on the use of the european globalisation adjustment fund. since the fund was set up in 2006, we have always abstained from voting because we were against the fund from the beginning, as we do not believe that the fund is the right instrument to help workers affected by structural changes. the fund has now grown, evolved and received its own allocation. it exists and will not disappear for the foreseeable future. we have therefore now decided to take a case-by-case view of whether they meet the very strict requirements for co-financing and long-term employment measures imposed on applicants. in the above case, we considered that the applications fulfilled these requirements. we will continue to support more effective and long-term instruments in the framework of budgetary and long-term budgetary work to create more jobs, respond to structural change and increase labour market flexibility.
in writing. - we voted today in favour of seven reports on the use of the european globalisation adjustment fund. since the fund was set up in 2006, we have always abstained from voting because we were against the fund from the beginning, as we do not believe that the fund is the right instrument to help workers affected by structural changes. the fund has now grown, evolved and received its own allocation. it exists and will not disappear for the foreseeable future. we have therefore now decided to take a case-by-case view of whether they meet the very strict requirements for co-financing and long-term employment measures imposed on applicants. in the above case, we considered that the applications fulfilled these requirements. we will continue to support more effective and long-term instruments in the framework of budgetary and long-term budgetary work to create more jobs, respond to structural change and increase labour market flexibility.
in writing. - parliament voted in december on seven reports on the use of the european globalisation adjustment fund. since 2009, i have voted against all proposals to use the fund as a rule. this is because i do not think that the fund is the right instrument to help workers affected by structural change. the fund has now grown, evolved and received its own allocation. it will therefore exist for the foreseeable future. so it is a matter of making the best of the situation. to benefit from the fund, applicants must meet very strict requirements for co-financing and long-term employment measures. in view of this, i have therefore voted in favour of all the reports in question. this does not change my view that the creation of the fund was more than questionable and that it should be wound up. europe and the eu can respond to competition and structural changes in the market in much better, more efficient and more sustainable ways in the long term. but now it's time to, as they say, make amends.
in writing. - we swedish social democrats are in favour of a global transaction tax. the under-taxation of the financial sector is a structural problem that needs to be addressed. since a global tax is unlikely to be introduced in the near future, we have been in favour of europe taking the lead in the debate over the last few years with a tax at eu level. since that option also proved politically impossible last summer, the point is that only 11 member states will now jointly establish such a taxation. we have nothing in principle against deeper cooperation. however, we have found it problematic that at the time of this decision parliament did not have a clear picture of the concrete regulatory framework that the 11 partner countries will eventually apply. for example, the commission has not yet presented an analysis of the possible negative impact of the tax on those countries - including sweden - which initially chose to opt out of the cooperation. because of these ambiguities, we have decided to abstain from this vote.
in writing. − we support the ambitious report on fundamental rights in the eu 20102011 which was voted through. however, we do disagree on a few points. the report criticises the commission for not having tabled a legislative proposal on media freedom. while we support measures to protect press freedom in the eu, we do not, however, in line with the european union of journalists, consider that a common eu law is a good way to achieve this. we also do not support the call on the council to seek a high level of harmonisation in the work on the new personal data protection regulation. this would risk overriding sector-specific national legislation and result in a lower level of privacy protection.
in writing. - we abstained in the final vote on the situation of fundamental rights in the european union (2010-2011). the commission has already submitted a proposal for a regulation on the protection of the environment. we voted against the one put forward by our group because it excluded taking positions on issues such as gender equality. for example, it did not clearly point to the existence of sexual discrimination which is clearly present in the union. the vote on the main resolution provided an opportunity to draw attention to problems which we believe are global and european in their individual aspects. however, in the final vote we were unable to support the resolution because, contrary to our view, it was far too broad. the concept of fundamental human rights risks losing its significance if it is accompanied by too many political positions which are simply part of national policy. however, we are particularly concerned that the resolution addresses in many points directly, but mostly indirectly, matters which clearly fall within the competence of the member states and which in some cases are contrary to the treaty of lisbon. the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the protection of the environment and the environment from harmful substances.
in writing. - it is hardly reasonable to conflate fundamental principles of human rights and freedoms with the voluntary and self-serving wishes of all to improve european society. respect for human rights and freedoms should, in my view, be synonymous with respect for the values enshrined in the charter of the united nations and the universal declaration of human rights. it should also be recalled in this context that not all legislation is to be transferred to the european parliament and the commission. the principle of subsidiarity must be respected and the responsibilities which the treaties confer on nations should not be reflected in the text of resolutions as being or becoming the tasks of the eu. that is why i abstained from voting on the report today.
the committee on development and cooperation has been asked to submit an opinion. - mr president! - what is it? the declaration of human rights is the most important international treaty we have. we know that the countries of the world community have signed and pledged to uphold these human rights and freedoms. however, we are well aware of - and have referred to it in several posts here - the trampling and neglect of respect for human rights and freedoms. syria has been mentioned, and we all know what the situation is there. eastern congo may have been a little too little or a lot too little. three hours ago we also heard about the situation in iran. let us make sure that this is not just a matter of talking about human rights and freedoms, but that we actually underline in our agreements that we demand something from those with whom we have relations or agreements in the field of human rights and freedoms. i am very pleased that we have a special representative for human rights and freedoms here in the european parliament and i am convinced that it is the best way for individual nations to have such a special representative.

mr president, i would like to thank you. we are ready to continue our engagement in ukraine, but ukraine's continued political and economic integration with the eu must be based on respect for our common values. these values have no place for politically motivated legal proceedings or obstacles to the opposition's participation in elections. there are a number of areas that ukraine needs to work on. this concerns in particular the compatibility with international standards of parliamentary elections, the implementation of reforms and selective justice. ukraine's ability to deliver in these areas will be crucial to the scope and depth of the eu's engagement with ukraine. we expect the ukrainian government to immediately engage in an inclusive dialogue with the opposition and to establish an electoral system based on reliability and clear rules. we also expect the necessary reforms to be implemented to make legal processes more numerous, transparent and independent. ukraine has many friends in the european parliament. that is why we must closely monitor what is happening and what steps ukraine is taking. i would also like to thank the commissioner for his incredibly great commitment to ukraine in particular and his almost personal commitment to the country. i would ask the commissioner to continue to keep us informed of ukraine's progress. if ukraine shows significant progress in the areas i have just mentioned, the council is ready to sign the association agreement as early as next year in vilnius. we want to cooperate with ukraine. we have chosen ukraine, but now ukraine must do its part and show that europe is ukraine's choice.
the s&d group is in favour of the proposal. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. i would like to begin by thanking the rapporteur, tatjana ždanoka, for her excellent and inclusive cooperation. the eu agency for fundamental rights is a hugely important tool for those of us who stand up for the rights of eu citizens and not just for a single internal market. through its expertise and well-founded reports and analyses of legislative proposals, the agency makes an enormous contribution to our legislative work and, by extension, to a europe where fundamental rights mean something in practice. only by gathering independent knowledge can we work against all the violations that actually occur in the eu. this report on a multiannual framework programme for the agency for fundamental rights, which will guide the work of the agency from 2013 to 2017, is therefore of paramount importance. a major problem for my political group has been that police cooperation and cooperation in criminal matters, which have become part of eu policy since the entry into force of the lisbon treaty, are not explicitly covered by the agency's mandate. this means that we cannot benefit from the agency's help in the often sensitive work in this area. the resistance of the member states is regrettable. the s&d group initially did not want to approve an agreement on a multiannual framework programme which does not include police cooperation. however, as this could mean that the agency would be without a mandate after the end of the year, we nevertheless decided to approve the report. at the same time, i cannot hide the fact that i find it extremely regrettable that the council of ministers is not able to complete its work in time. i would particularly like to call on those member states which are trying to prevent a far-reaching mandate for the agency and for fundamental rights to rethink and show more responsibility for the rights of their citizens.
mr president, i would like to thank you. we know quite well how the crisis has arisen. it started with the unregulated financial market in the united states that we imported into our country. then there was the non-functioning stability pact for the euro countries which was our self-produced crisis factor. there are, of course, ways out of this crisis. the member states must be supported by decisions taken at different levels in the eu. we have to use the fact that there are a lot of available resources in the economy. we need to use it for long-term investments, especially in those that are important for the climate and the environment. it's a factor that gives us growth. it's a factor that gives us long-term vision. it is a factor that prevents us from falling into other serious crises. however, the ultimate issue of climate is not just political goals, but also political-social goals. with the posting of workers directive we broke down the trade union rights of employees in europe. we cannot compete with china or with other parts of the world with low wages, with things that are not climate-smart production. we have to give people their dignity back. the path to growth in europe is through having a skilled workforce that is well paid for the work they do and that has strong trade union rights. it's very high on the agenda.
for the verts/ale group. mr president, i would like to thank you for your reply. the caste system is an old system of oppression that has no place in our century. the dalits and adivasis are discriminated against, and they are made both casteless and lawless, even though this kind of discrimination is prohibited, which also leads to serious violence against women. it is also time to demand that our own companies, the textile industry, do not contribute to discrimination and oppression. but just so you in the audience understand what we're talking about, i'm gonna tell you that i'm probably the only person in this room who emptied a latrine manually this summer. i did it in person only twice at my own summer home, but it was certainly not a pleasant experience. imagine spending ten hours a day emptying train latrines manually. it's a terrible work that is forbidden and should be eradicated. there are other ways to take care of our own feces.
